<insert>
White spots <note>original: "Albule"; likely referring to cataracts or corneal opacities</note> of the eyes are struck out <note>original: "excutiuntur"; this refers to the surgical technique of 'couching' where a needle is used to displace the clouded lens</note> in this manner;
</insert>

<column-break/>

->**On the Treatment of the Eyes**<-

The procedure for clearing the vision involves a steady hand and a sharp needle. When the white film <note>the cataract</note> has hardened within the eye, the physician must carefully depress it so that it falls away from the field of vision. This ancient practice, known as couching, requires the patient to remain still while the obstruction is moved downward, restoring a measure of sight to the darkened eye.

<summary>This page describes the surgical procedure for treating cataracts, known as couching, where a needle is used to displace the clouded lens of the eye. It serves as a significant historical record of medieval ophthalmological practices and surgical techniques.</summary>

<keywords>cataract surgery, couching, ophthalmology, medieval medicine, eye treatment, surgical needle</keywords>

The surgeon uses a mallet and a specialized chisel to remove the growth, while the patient holds a basin to collect the blood. This scene is characteristic of the "Chirurgia" (Surgery) by Roger Frugardi, a foundational 12th-century medical text that emphasized practical, hands-on intervention.</note>

<meta>This page begins the treatise on the herb Betony, attributed to Antonius Musa, physician to Emperor Augustus. It follows the introductory frontispiece of the herbal manuscript.</meta>

# Page 13

**A**ntonius Musa, <gloss>physician</gloss>, sends greetings to Maecenas and Agrippa. To Caesar Augustus, the most outstanding of all mortals, <gloss>he was able to know</gloss> <gloss>he will be able</gloss> by his own judgment <gloss>he will be able</gloss> by the nearest help <gloss>through</gloss> the most famous of my arts. By the judgment of Caesar, <gloss>I might be able</gloss> to be present with you, because through this commentary on my art I <gloss>wish</gloss> a small gift to reach you. Therefore, you yourself, Maecenas and Agrippa, will command me to deserve <gloss>worthily</gloss> from you. This work <gloss>from use and knowledge</gloss> is the fruit of my experience, evolved from all the disciplines of the physicians' goals. Always having me with you as if I were present.

Musa, when I proposed to speak of medicines, I considered it a kind of sacrilege <gloss>to exercise these powers</gloss> to pass over the effect of the herb Betony <note>original: "uetonice"</note>. <gloss>This herb</gloss>, whose healthfulness is so suited to bodies and other things. In every <gloss>science, however</gloss>, its powers and remedies must be spoken of first. For such a great thing is to be looked upon by the hand, which without <gloss>theory</gloss> profits the table of the stomach; it offers more than just a taste to the sight. The remedies for the sick are composed with a certain moderate gentleness, prepared so as not to cause a loathing of sweetness, if it provides offense through sharpness. Therefore, they are accustomed to be beneficial by attaining the effect of health. These, like aloe or wormwood, of which we do not <gloss>thus</gloss> feel the help so much as you feel the bitterness. Betony is so powerful that when those who are suffering take it <gloss>having smelled it gradually before</gloss>, they will even love it gladly, as if you were compelling one who is ignorant to suspect a herb useful to his body, since its effect can remedy as many as seven or [more] diseases. Although it can be done, it profits everyone, but it is especially suitable for healing <gloss>and qualities</gloss> <gloss>although the theory</gloss> women. Therefore, I think it should be placed first for those whom the following healthful things profit. However, few physicians have known this herb Betony, and those who do know it <gloss>namely for their own profit</gloss> do not persevere in finding it; perhaps <gloss>to this one there are</gloss> those who knew it to be useful in its powers <gloss>to a few they were unknown, therefore by right it [is so]</gloss>. Which is <gloss>just as</gloss> the hydraulic organs <gloss>it was</gloss> which no one is ignorant of. But I did not think it necessary to speak longer, so that the herb Betony alone might be known to be effective. You shall gather this herb in its proper time: that is, in the month of August, when other herbs begin to ripen. You shall shake it out with its seed and roots without using iron <note>Ancient and medieval herbalists often believed iron tools "corrupted" the magical or medicinal potency of certain plants.</note> so that it does not cling to the earth, and you shall dry it in the shade; and thus its powers with its roots, having been reduced very softly into a powder by an aromatic sieve. When you wish to use it, use it thus:

**I**t is necessary to know the weight or medicinal weights:
**D**rachma <note>original: "Dragma"</note>: is the weight of a silver denarius, that is, three scruples. <gloss>That is</gloss> a bolus: the sixth part of a drachma, and that is half a scruple. A quarter is: ten drachmas, that is: 30 scruples. <gloss>That is</gloss> an acetabulum <note>A Roman liquid measure, literally a "vinegar cup."</note>: is 15 drachmas, that is 45 scruples. A spoonful <note>original: "Coclearum"</note>: is half a drachma, that is: half a scruple. <gloss>A mina:</gloss> 100 drachmas, that is: 300 scruples.

<insert>Betony</insert>
<insert>Betony</insert>
<insert>Betony</insert>

<meta>This page continues the pharmacological instructions from the Herbarium of Pseudo-Apuleius, focusing on the extensive medicinal uses of Betony (Betonica) and beginning the section on Plantain (Plantago/Arnoglossa). The text maintains the structure of specific ailments followed by herbal preparations.</meta>

# Betony: How it is used, <unclear>as some say</unclear>

<margin>For those thrown from a vehicle</margin>
Take the herb betony in a drink: for those thrown from a vehicle <note>likely referring to falls from carriages or horses</note>.
Three drams <term>drachma: a unit of weight, approx. 3.4 grams</term> of the herb betony: in two cyathi <term>cyathus: a liquid measure, approx. 45ml</term> of old wine: let him drink it warmed: we are accustomed to drink it.

<margin>For carbuncles</margin>
The herb betony and one to three cyathi: frequently applied: we have experienced it to be effective.

<margin>For the jaundiced, that is, those with the royal disease who are golden-colored</margin>
The herb betony and three cyathi of urine <note>The use of urine in medieval medicine was common for both diagnosis and as a carrier for herbs</note> frequently applied: we have experienced it to be effective.

<margin>For those with spleen complaints</margin>
One dram of the herb betony: he shall give two cyathi of hot water to drink: <unclear>eoncan cum</unclear>.

<margin>Also concerning the same</margin>
<note>original: "Varnungia"</note> Applied to a short wound, it heals it changeably.

<margin>For rubbing/pains in the kidneys</margin>
One ounce of the herb betony <unclear>and one</unclear> three cyathi; let him drink for three days, he will be healed quickly.

<margin>For those weary from travel</margin>
One dram of the herb betony in three cyathi of three-month-old wine; you shall give it as a drink.

<margin>For those suffering from stones</margin>
A dram of the herb betony mixed with three cyathi of water; it shall be given; it removes the aversion to food.

<margin>If the teeth are chattering, it removes the aversion</margin>
Three drams of the herb betony, one ounce of honey; boil the honey until it hardens; give two cyathi of hot water for him to drink: it is very beneficial.

<margin>For those who cannot digest</margin>
Four drams of the herb betony with one ounce of cooked honey; they shall make lozenges <term>pastillos: small medicinal cakes or troches</term> from it the size of a hazelnut. And let him eat one lozenge twice a day for up to three days: and give one cyathus of hot water for him to drink.

<margin>For swelling of the nerves</margin>
The herb betony or with honey and ground in one; you shall foment the area; it can be applied around it beforehand.

<margin>For one who has taken poison</margin>
Three drams of the herb betony: four cyathi of wine: give it to him to drink: he will immediately vomit the poison and be freed.

---

<margin>For the bite of a serpent</margin>
Three drams of the herb betony: three cyathi of wine: give it to him to drink: he will immediately vomit the poison and be freed.

<margin>Also for the bites of serpents</margin>
Six drams of the herb betony: three cyathi of dark wine: <unclear>amari lintrū</unclear> place the medicine over the wound.

<margin>For the bite of a mad dog</margin>
Indeed, for the bite of a mad dog, apply the herb itself crushed alone to the path of the bite of the mad dog: it will heal wonderfully.

<margin>For fistulas</margin>
The herb veronica <gloss>betony</gloss> <note>The scribe or a later annotator clarifies that 'veronica' here refers to betony</note> crushed: they shall make little cakes <unclear>turtundelas</unclear> and apply them together: and bound quickly, it purges the filth.

<margin>For swellings and pain in the eyes</margin>
Two drams of the herb betony: let him drink it with honey-water <term>mulsum: wine sweetened with honey</term> if he has no fever. If he has a fever: with hot water: we believe it heals the pain of the <unclear>liborum</unclear>.

---

<margin>For gout</margin>
The herb veronica <gloss>betony</gloss> for gout <term>podagra: specifically gout of the foot</term>: boil it down to a third part in water and give it as a drink: it is beneficial. If the ear <unclear>fite eta</unclear>.

<margin>For the ear <unclear>fite eta</unclear></margin>
<note>This section contains highly corrupted Latin or specialized jargon regarding the "Platonic" tradition of medicine</note>
...they affirm a gentle hope for the color: and the plant <unclear>placourū</unclear>...

<margin>For freckles/spots</margin>
Many have handed down to the public a few types of herbs for the variations of bodies, mindful of the faith of truth: so that they might call the stupidity of words most perfect: because a potion is sold by doctors rather than a cure... they seek payment from the dead. However much they do, it is nothing. They look for an opportunity to prolong the time of treatment for easier profit. I think they are older than the diseases themselves. Let us propose a remedy for the eyes: which either lives or is most conducive... to my people... and to foreigners whom some bodily vexation has reached: let our written knowledge invite them...
The letter of Plato <note>Referring to the fictionalized "Epistle of Plato" often prefacing these herbals</note> regarding the care of his people ends here.
The plant of this herb is Plantain.

---

It is called wild Arnoglossa <note>Greek: lamb's tongue</note>: <term>Arnoglossa</term>: <term>Tifer</term>: it is said to be a protection.
Others call it: <term>arnoglossa</term>: <term>Arnorıh</term>: <term>eptapleuron</term> <note>Greek: seven-ribbed</term>: Others call it: <term>charibidolopiō</term>: <term>minicanpane</term>: The Spanish call it: <term>dycaricim</term>: <term>fıcultı</term>: <term>polyneuronehon</term> <note>Greek: many-nerved</term>: The Prophets call it: <term>vracneunionos</term>: The Egyptians call it: <term>Afaer</term>: Others call it: <term>thecarioy</term>: The Dacians call it: <term>simpote</term>: The Italians call it: <term>plantagola</term>: The Romans call it: <term>plantago maior</term> <note>Greater Plantain</note>.
Others: <term>Sepa nernia</term>. It grows well in marshes and in meadows and in moist places.

<margin>For headache</margin>
The root of the plantain herb suspended from the neck removes a headache wonderfully.

<margin>For pain in the kidneys</margin>
The herb plantain, if it is used as a poultice: it takes away the pain. And if there is a swelling: then crushed and applied: it easily takes away the swelling.

<margin>For the appearance of the eyes</margin>
The herb plantain made into a drink takes away the redness of all eyes and most perfectly cures the <unclear>chauer</unclear> of men.

<margin>For difficult menstruation</margin>
The leaves of the plantain herb wonderfully prepared: and boil with lentils: give it to her to eat: it will immediately bind the veins.

<margin>For those who spit up pus</margin>
The herb plantain crushed and the juice expressed: give its juice to him to drink: they will be healed wonderfully.

<margin>For wounds</margin>
The seed of the plantain herb for all <unclear>amfuum</unclear>. And made into a powder and sprinkled on wounds: it will heal wonderfully. The same herb crushed and applied: it cools places that are burned by excessive heat and heals them most perfectly.

<meta>This page continues the herbal tradition of Pseudo-Apuleius, focusing on the medicinal properties of Plantain and Cinquefoil. The text follows the standard format of naming the plant, listing its synonyms in various languages, and providing specific recipes for ailments.</meta>

# The Herb Plantain
<note>Latin: *Herba Plantago*. A common weed used since antiquity for its cooling and healing properties.</note>

Drinking the herb plantain in vinegar or wine brings on menstruation. It also binds the belly <note>i.e., treats diarrhea</note> against wind.

## Against the bite of a serpent
The herb plantain terrifies the serpent. If its juice is taken with wine and applied on top, or if the same herb is bound to the wound, it resists all venom.

<margin>If anything from a mouse [bite] <unclear>...</unclear></margin>

---

## Herb against the sting of a scorpion
If you apply the crushed root of the plantain to that place, it heals wonderfully; we have experienced this frequently.

## For worms
You shall crush the herb plantain and give one spoonful of the extracted juice to drink. If you place the red herb itself on the navel, it kills the worms in a short time and repels them outward.

## If there should be any hardness in the body
The herb plantain, ground with animal fat <term>axungia: rendered animal fat, usually pig lard, used as a base for ointments</term> without salt, and the juice made into a <term>malagma: a softening poultice or plaster</term>; apply this and all hardness disperses.

## For five-day fevers
You shall give the juice of the plantain herb to drink in honey-water <note>Latin: *aqua mulsa*</note> before the hour of the fever's onset; it has a wonderful effect.

## For gout and all pains of the tendons
<note>Text obscured in the gutter of the manuscript</note>

---

The leaves of the plantain herb, crushed and applied with a little salt and catnip <note>Latin: *nepeta*</note>, is the best thing to do for an onset [of pain].

## For growths
Crush three roots and give them to drink with wine or with water in wine just before the onset [of fever].

## For secondary bile
The seed of the plantain herb, crushed and given as a drink, is certain to be beneficial.

## For recent wounds
The herb plantain, crushed with old animal fat and applied, heals miraculously.

## If from a journey
If the feet have swollen from a journey, the herb plantain is crushed with vinegar and applied; it takes away the swelling.

If a wound has occurred near the eyes or inside the nose, express the juice of the plantain herb and apply it with soft soaked wool for nine days; it is effectively healed.

## For distortions of the limbs
Crush the seed of the plantain herb; give it to drink with pearl barley in warm wine. It is made healthy immediately.

## For "pirreonas"
<note>Likely referring to *pyriasis* or a type of inflammatory redness/burn.</note>
The herb plantain, crushed and applied with animal fat, is believed to heal miraculously.

## For ulcers of the mouth
Hold the juice of the plantain herb in the mouth, or let the patient chew its leaves; it is healed in a wonderful way.

## For healing fistulas
Pour the juice of the plantain herb over fistulas for many days, and it will be healed.

## For bladder difficulties
The roots or leaves of the plantain herb, crushed and given in a drink, will cause the urine to be expelled three times.

## For the bite of a "contrabru"
<note>Likely a corruption of a word for a specific insect or small animal.</note>
The herb plantain, crushed and applied, easily heals the bite.

---

->The "Sun-Turn" <note>Heliotrope</note>, that is<-
->Dove's Foot<-

# Name of the herb Pentaphillon
<note>Cinquefoil (Five-Leaved Grass). The following section lists its names in various ancient languages, reflecting the international nature of medieval medicine.</note>

The Greeks call it *pseudo-silinon*. The Dacians <note>People of ancient Romania/Balkans</note> call it *caltupetalon* and some call it *selecon*. Others call it *aspaleton*. The Gauls call it *innatius*. The Jews call it *esophy*. Others call it *chebeoris*, others call it *callipetalon*. The Italians call it *pylizon*. Others call it *petracymon* <note>Rock-parsley</note>. Others call it *cynacien*. The Egyptians call it *chruphi*. The <unclear>...</unclear> call it *theonethron*. The Romans call it *quinquefolium* <note>Five-leaf</note>. The Moors call it *manuusoyer* or "Hand of Mars." Others call it *pimpedonum*. The Spaniards call it *camprocila*. It grows in shady and trampled places, or in meadows. It is soft to the touch and harsh to the taste.

The herb for the joints, or the herb "five-leaf": if one has been struck, [apply it] with its juice and old animal fat without salt; it heals most perfectly.

## For the heat of Venus
<note>Likely referring to venereal heat or inflammation.</note>
The herb five-leaf, crushed and the juice pressed out, if drunk beforehand without food, heals the pain of the stomach, or the tongue, or the throat.

---

Reduce the herb five-leaf to a powder. Mixed with honey, rub the mouth with it, or the tongue, or even the windpipe or the throat; it immediately purges all foulness.

## For headache
Circle the herb five-leaf three times with the middle finger, and afterwards, having picked and crushed it, bind it to the head; it heals effectively.

The herb five-leaf, when bound on, heals [the bite of] a mouse.

## For blood from the tongue or nostrils
The herb five-leaf, taken as a powder or its juice given as a drink, or its <unclear>...</unclear> for menstruation: it restrains the blood.

## For the "shivers"
<note>Latin: *auge rindrum*, likely referring to the chills of a fever.</note>
The herb five-leaf, crushed: give three measures <term>cyathos: a small liquid measure, about 0.045 liters</term> to drink. You will find relief in the third [hour/dose].

## For "epierri"
<note>Possibly a corruption of *erysipelas* (a skin infection) or *epinyctis* (sores).</note>
Crush the herb five-leaf with old wine and grind it together with vinegar, without salt. Sprinkle it from a wooden [vessel]; do not sprinkle it with wine alone. Apply the medicament as a true [cure] for *epieri*.

## For a burn
Many affirm that the herb five-leaf, crushed and applied, is beneficial.

## For the bite of a serpent
The herb five-leaf, crushed and its juice pressed out and given as a drink, heals miraculously and resists all poison.

---

### Name of the herb Columbaris
<note>Vervain or Pigeon-grass.</note>
By the Greeks it is called *iberoberanen*; others call it *peresterion*, others call it *dioferum*. The Moors call it *erigonon*. It is called *sanguinaria* <note>Blood-wort</note> by some, and *pereromo* by others. Others call it *callicfii*. The Romans call it *sideritis*. Others call it *cypralus*. The <unclear>...</unclear> call it *phiphonion*. The Jews call it *demetria*. Others call it *planchroman*. The Egyptians call it *pempethai*.

<summary>This page details the medicinal uses of Plantain and Cinquefoil (Five-Leaf Grass), providing recipes for snakebites, fevers, wounds, and internal ailments. It includes an extensive list of plant synonyms in various ancient languages including Greek, Dacian, Egyptian, and "Moorish."</summary>

<margin>To heal those with bladder stones:</margin>
**T**he juice of the herb Vervain, given to drink with wine, honey, water, and bile—let him drink it with this prayer: he will be healed immediately.

**T**he herb Vervain, dried and covered in a small pouch, then reduced to powder over a strong fire: you shall give five spoonfuls mixed with the best wine; taken as a drink for three days, it wonderfully eradicates the ailment and is beneficial. And for others, you shall give it according to their strength.

**T**he root of the herb Vervain, crushed and given with much of the best warm liquid, incredibly aids those with stones. If there is anything seen to impede the urine, it quickly brings it forth to be drawn out.

<margin>For snake bites:</margin>

**Wild** <term>Henbane</term> <note>original: "hyosciamu"</note> it is called. The Italians call it *Lactercula*. The Romans call it *Simphonia* or *Symca*. Others call it *Prionion*. The Virici: *Calaclitem*. The Ensentici: *Chambrion*. The Prophets: *Sacrinaron*. The Iflanes: *Gilion*. The Erastrif: *Eyfomon*. The Persians: *Ufamum*. The Egyptians: *Citu*. The Gauls call it *Bellinoten*. It grows in cultivated and sandy places, or in the tracks of roads among debris. There is another kind with reddish, very foul, and poisonous leaves. That one which has white leaves: use these properties.

The first, with wine, is for ear pain.

The juice of the **Symphoniaca** <note>original: "Berbe symphoniace"; likely Henbane (Hyoscyamus niger)</note> mixed with rose oil and dropped into the ears; it wonderfully takes away the pain of the ears. If there are worms, it will kill them. **For swelling of the knees or shins; if a wound has caused you swelling;**

The herb **Symphoniaca** pounded with sheep dung and a little vinegar; made into a kind of emollient <term>malagma: a softening medicinal plaster or poultice</term> and applied; it takes away the swelling. **For toothache;**

The root of the herb **Symphoniaca** boiled in harsh wine; let it be held in the mouth; on account of the toothache, the pain will be taken away. **For pains of the groin;**

The root of the herb **Symphoniaca** bound immediately upon the thigh; it takes away excessive pain. **For pains of the feet;**

The herb **Symphoniaca** likewise bound upon the feet; it takes away the pain of the feet, provided it is placed on top of the feet. **For pain of the sinews and affliction of the lungs;**

The juice of the herb **Symphoniaca** drunk warm with water will be wonderfully healed. **For healing the internal organs of women;**

...it is a wonder how quickly it causes a loosening of the bowels.

### The name of this herb is Proserpinaca <note>Knotgrass; likely *Polygonum aviculare*</note>.

### Among the Greeks: it is called Poligonos.

Others call it: *Cynosiamen* <note>Dog's-brain</note>. Others: *Polygona cos*. Others: *Apilatiou*. Others: *Poligar pon*. Others: *Caronon*. Others: *Ceeinppo dion*. The Egyptians call it: *Outeopetalon*. Others: *Zecham*. Others: *Onegaffion*. However, the Egyptians truly call it <unclear>...</unclear> *Moros*: *Peut frem*. <unclear>...</unclear> *Rophe*: *Gonos henotios* <note>Greek: "productive of offspring"</note> <unclear>...</unclear> *n uici*: *Zunirius*. The Romans call it: *Sanguinalis* <note>Blood-herb</note>. Others call it: *Siconariam*. Others: *Sertucam*. Others: *Scorpinacem*. It grows everywhere in cultivated places, and on embankments or rocks. You may gather it at any time.

<margin>In English <note>This likely refers to the common name "Knotgrass"</note></margin>

---

**F**or pain, if you frequently anoint the area with the juice of the herb Proserpinaca mixed with rose oil, it takes away the pain in a wonderful way. **For pain in the breasts that have milk and are swollen:**

**T**he herb Proserpinaca, well-kneaded with wheat flour and butter, disperses the pain and removes the swelling in a wonderful way. **For defects or fears of the eyes:**

**G**rasp the herb Proserpinaca before sunrise or sunset, and draw a circle around it with a gold ring. Say what was written at the beginning <note>referring to a specific charm or prayer</note>; go the next day before sunrise and take it. If it is hung around the neck, it will be diligently beneficial. **For earache:**

**F**or earache, the juice of the herb Proserpinaca is restorative; when put into the ear, it wonderfully restores the pain <note>meaning it cures the source of the pain</note>; it also heals the sores that grow in the ears. **For those who are bow-legged <term>deuaricatos: those with spreading or distorted limbs</term>:**

**G**ive the juice of the herb Proserpinaca to drink in warm water, as much as suffices; it is made effective. **For quartan fever <note>a fever that recurs every four days, often associated with malaria</note>:**

**G**ive the herb Polygonum to drink with seven or nine peppercorns during a bowel movement; it is preferred to gather it on a Thursday during a waning moon, and give it while the patient is lying on their back. **For the "superfluity" of women <note>excessive menstrual bleeding</note>:**

**I**f he gives this potion, she will be wonderfully freed. You shall charm it thus with the little herb Proserpinaca: "From the daughter of a brief king, whom you now pray for as most lame, thus you close [the flow]..." <note>This appears to be a rhythmic healing incantation or "historiola" involving a mythological figure</note>.

---

->The First Cure<-

<margin>For passing blood in the urine</margin>

**T**ake the juice of the herb Proserpinaca made with the best harsh wine; give it to drink on an empty stomach for nine days. You will experience a wonderful thing.

**For pains of the loins.**

### The name of this herb is Aristolochia <note>Birthwort; from the Greek for "excellent birth"</note>

The Greeks call it: *Partonum*. Others: *Drazam*. Others: *Coelecarpon*. Others: *Regerennum*. Others: *Epesiam*. Others: *Eleliticem*. Others: *Flanes*. The Dacians call it: <unclear>...</unclear>

---

Others: *Sociam*. The Egyptians: *Sophoerph*. <unclear>...</unclear> *Camelum*. The Italians: *Cremalu*.

### The Dacians call it: Wild Wormwood.

It grows in mountainous and sunny places, and among crags or stony ground.

<margin>Round and long Aristolochia</margin>

->The name of this is Round Aristolochia<-

**T**he herb Aristolochia for healing fistulas <note>abnormal passages in the body</note>: when well-cleansed, it heals and leads the fistulas back to health. Indeed, physicians do nothing without it. **For shivering from cold.**

<margin>Otherwise: Let the place near the nose be anointed in the same family <note>possibly referring to a related treatment for the face</note>.</margin>

**T**he herb Aristolochia boiled in oil has a warming power. **For infants, if they have been troubled:** Take the herb Aristolochia and use it to fumigate the infant <note>burning the herb so the smoke surrounds the child</note>; you will immediately make the infants cheerful. **For cancerous sores that grow in the nostrils.**

**W**hen you mix the herb Aristolochia with cypress, female dragonwort, and honey, you shall cleanse and heal the sores. **Against the strikes of serpents and the bites of men.**

**B**oil one denarius weight <note>a Roman weight unit, approx. 3.9 grams</note> of the root of the herb Aristolochia, crushed, in one *emina* <note>approx. 0.25 liters</note> of liquid; let it be drunk often. It immediately drives away all poisons.

### The name of this herb is Hasturace and Epessos. In English <note>likely Starwort or a similar composite</note>.

**F**or the power of holding oneself together: give crushed or powdered Aristolochia; it discusses <note>disperses</note> all poison or fear; also for infants if they have been troubled. **For very sharp fevers: likewise for fistulas.**

**D**ry the herb Aristolochia in abundance and use it for fumigation under the bed where an infant lies ill; it takes away the slipperiness of the womb <note>likely referring to preventing miscarriage or prolapse</note>, puts demons to flight, and wards off all bites.

<meta>This page continues the herbal tradition of Pseudo-Apuleius, following the previous page's discussion of botanical remedies. The catchword "moniminis" (monuments/tombs) connects to the description of where the herb Ostriago grows.</meta>

### The name of this herb is Chamaelea. It is called "vres" here. <note>original: "Cameelea". Likely Chamaeleon or Ground Pine.</note>

<margin>LG</margin> <note>Likely a shorthand for a language or source reference.</note>
<margin>Chamomile</margin> <note>original: "Cameele"</note>

<margin>In English.</margin>
Some call it Crocodile <note>original: "Corodrilum"</note>. **Others** call it Hypica. The Prophets call it: Onocardi. The Egyptians: Seneu. The Sicilians: Aphrodisos. **Others:** Emeleuca.

**Others:** Libirum of Venus. **Others:** Cierilatheos. **Others:** Staticeum. **Others:** Adonition. The Spaniards: Memonum. The Danes: Scam. **Others:** Cieritheos. The Persians <note>original: "Miser", likely referring to 'Misr' or Egypt/East</note>: Halpo.

**For liver ailments** <note>original: "Ad epatericos"</note>
**T**he juice of the herb Chamaelea given as a drink with wine; for those suffering from fever: you shall give it to drink with warm water. It will liberate them wonderfully. **Against poison.**
**T**ake the herb Chamaelea, dried and reduced to a powder. Give it to drink mixed with wine: it will cast out all poison by rejection <note>i.e., by vomiting</note>. **For jaundice** <note>original: "Ad ycteri"</note>
**T**he herb Chamaelea—that is, *pvocura uidafne*, *Camelris*, *camepittia* <note>Ground Pine</note>—weighed in equal parts, ground and very finely sifted. Put this into a drink with wine; you will find it beneficial. Give five spoonfuls to drink for a man, three for a woman, and for children, one spoonful. It will profit them wonderfully.

### The name of this herb is Chamaelephne. <note>Spurge Laurel</note>
The Greeks call it Daphnites. **Others** indeed call it Carogagof. The Italians call it Laurimerris: this is the wild variety. **Others:** Fubeis.

**For skin blemishes and for settling the stomach.**
**T**he herb Chamaelephne reduced to a powder, very finely, and given as a drink in warm water: it settles the stomach.

### The name of this herb: Ostriago. <note>Possibly a species of Scabious or Sideritis</note>

Others indeed call it: Sartis murtis.
**I**t grows around monuments, or in tombs, or on walls that are around mountains. **For all wounds.**
**T**ake the roots of the herb Ostriago and crush them strongly: and place them over every wound. You will experience a miraculous thing.
**Against all ulcers.**
**T**he herb Ostriago, crushed and applied, heals most excellently all ulcers that arise on the body. If you wish to uproot this herb, you should uproot it at dawn and before sunrise. You shall gather it in the month of July.

### The name of this herb is Britannica. <note>Water Dock or similar marsh plant</note>
**Others:** *circis haby septicos*. *Votoof* calls it: "similar to flames." **Others:** *phere fluuiorū*. The Romans: Britannica.

The Italians call it *Beaplanca uiuam*. Its primary cure is for ailments that arise in the mouth, like the herb Britannica and lettuce. This same life-giving juice should be expressed beforehand: let it be held in the mouth, and it will profit greatly thereafter. **For toothache:**
**I**f you do not have the herb Britannica green, have it dry and well-cooked with wine so that it reaches the thickness of honey. It provides a worthy effect. **For pain in the gums when it first arises.**
**T**he herb Britannica has a wonderful and greatest power for the teeth when pain arises. By applying it, it soothes. Or use its juice as a wash. **The herb for the same.**
**T**his herb dried and then the powder used for the same purpose with wine; it is certain to profit wonderfully. **The herb for loosening paralysis.**
**T**he herb Britannica green with its roots and myrtle seeds, applied for about two or three days: it profits much and heals. **To restrain the bowels.**
**T**he juice of the herb Britannica given as a drink by itself; it purges the belly without danger. **To treat the spleen.**
**T**he herb Britannica green with its roots crushed, and two or three cups of the best wine; you shall give it to be drunk. It will be able to profit greatly.
**For the throat-ailment which the Greeks call "syracie."** <note>Likely quinsy or a similar throat infection</note>
**T**he flower of the herb Britannica, gathered before it is heard by any living thing <note>a folk-magic ritual requirement for silence</note>, and swallowed; for a whole year the throat-ailment will not be found.

### The name of this herb is Wild Lettuce. <note>original: "Lactuca siluatica"</note>

<margin>Wild lettuce.</margin>
By the Greeks it is called: *tristidagru*. **Others:** *tideracion*. **Others:** *Aspoddoy*. **Others:** *pē*.

# [Wild Lettuce]

The Egyptians call this herb: *Ibonsor*. The Italians call it: **Wild Lettuce** <note>original: *Lactuca silvatica*</note>. It grows in cultivated and sandy places. It is said that when the eagle flies high, it eats Wild Lettuce so that it may see the nature of things clearly; it is used for dimness of the eyes.

<margin>For the sight.</margin>
Crush the leaves of the **Wild Lettuce herb**, and with the juice pressed from them, mixed with honey, sharply anoint your eyes; you will receive the greatest clarity.

Mix the juice of the **Wild Lettuce herb** with old wine, or use Fumitory <note>original: *Capnum*, likely *Fumaria officinalis*</note> which has been collected without smoke, mixed with honey and the gall of a vulture. Store this in a bronze flask. Use this, and you will find it to be a supreme medicine.

### The name of this herb: Agrimony.

The Greeks call it: *Bone*. Some call it: *Vnanunam*. The Romans: *Argemon*. Others: *Liburnia*. Others: *Greater Mullein*. Others: *Cordial*. The Tuscans: *Rucia*. Others call it: *Rummala*. Others: *Domit gathum*. The Syrians: *Samocola*. It grows in fields or around hedges.

<margin>Agrimony</margin>

---

<margin>A brief cure for the eyes: for spots or pains.</margin>

**The herb Agrimony**, if used as described; or if it be dry, mixed with warm water and smeared on the eyes: it quickly dissipates bruising and discoloration.

**This herb** for pains of the belly: Agrimony mixed with wine and carefully prepared is suitable. If it be dry, do the same by mixing it in lukewarm water.

**The herb Agrimony** for tumors <note>original: *tartos*, likely referring to hard swellings</note>: crushed and applied with animal fat <note>term: *axungia* (hog's lard used as a base for ointments)</note>, it wonderfully removes pains, tumors, or dislocations. For a wound from iron: the herb agrimony crushed with vinegar and applied heals most perfectly.

**For all abscesses** <note>original: *apostemata*</note> that grow in the body: the herb agrimony thoroughly crushed and applied opens and smooths all abscesses. For wounds that arise from filth: crushed agrimony applied heals the wounds. If it be dry, do the same in lukewarm water. For pains of the spleen: the herb agrimony used as above consumes the spleen <note>meaning: reduces a swollen spleen</note> most perfectly.

**The herb Agrimony** against poison: two drachms <note>term: *drachma* (a unit of weight, approx. 3.5 grams)</note> given in a drink with two small cups <note>term: *cyathus* (a small measure for liquid)</note> of wine dispels all poison.

### The name of this herb: Asphodel

The Greeks call it: *Polioreu*. Others: *Babuzrum*. The Italians: *Albucum*. Others: *Rabdo*. Others: *Stircum*. The Egyptians: *Asfount*. The Romans: *Albucum*. The Tuscans call it: *Ampullaria*. This herb carries two green marks with it. It is used for removals or for pains of the feet.

---

<margin>Hundred-headed. English: Asphodel.</margin>

Anoint with the juice of the **Asphodel herb** mixed with almond oil; it wonderfully heals every pain that is in the body.

**For the teeth** or for pains of the liver: boil the small roots of the **Asphodel herb** with honeyed water. When drunk, it wonderfully takes away the pain of the liver. The names of this herb: *Vstapattica*, *Vanteum*, *Narcissus*.

---

The Italians call it: Sharp Dock <note>original: *Lapatium acritum*</note>. The Romans: *Rumice*. Others: the same. Others: *Rumic lanciniam*. The Egyptians: <unclear>illegible</unclear>.

---

<margin>English: Centaury</margin>

The Greeks name it. It grows among thickets or everywhere. Its first stalks are <unclear>illegible</unclear>.

**The herb**, when it grows from the body <note>likely referring to the plant's growth habit</note>. Take it with bread as a meal. And then make a poultice <note>term: *malagma* (a soft, softening external application)</note> from it; then apply it, and it heals most perfectly. This is the **Greater Centaury**, also called the Herb of Chiron. There are two kinds of these.

It is said that the centaur Chiron found these two centauries; therefore, the name of the centaur himself was given to the herb. He himself established the medicine from these herbs and provided them to the sick.

The Greeks call it: *Agaronis*. Others: *Plectronam*. The Phoenicians: *Abussia*. Others: *Junneco*. Others: *Emetaclori*. Others call it: *Chironas*. Others: *Apogiostam*. Others: *Polidon*. Others: *Emercos*. The Egyptians: *Amiaud*.

# Hundred-heads, in English: Asphodel.

<margin>asphodel</margin>
**The herb** asphodel <note>original: "aspodili"</note>: if you rub the juice mixed with almond oil over the skin, it wonderfully heals every pain that has been in the body.

### For pain of the liver or the gallbladder.

<margin>the asphodel</margin>
**The herb** asphodel: boil the small roots with honeyed water. When drunk, it wonderfully takes away the pain of the liver or the gallbladder <note>The original uses both "iacoris" and "epatis," likely distinguishing between the organ and its associated ailments</note>. The name of this herb is: *Oxalapatum*, that is, Sharp Dock.

<margin>In English:</margin>
The Italians call it Sharp Dock <note>original: "Lapactum acutu"</note>. The Romans: Sorrel <note>original: "Rumice"</note>. Others: *Idiam*. Others: *Rumiz cantariam*. The Egyptians call it *Semem*.

---

The Egyptians call it *Semem*. It grows among the crops or everywhere. Its primary cure: if any hardness <note>likely a tumor or cyst</note> grows in the body, take sharp dock with old lard and domestic bread; from these make a softened mixture, like a poultice <note>original: "malagma"</note>, and apply it. You will heal it most perfectly.

# The name of this herb is Greater Centaury.

<margin>In English: Centaury</margin>
**There** are two kinds of this herb. It is said that Chiron the Centaur <note>A mythological figure known as the "wisest of all centaurs" and a tutor to heroes like Achilles; he was famed for his knowledge of medicine</note> discovered these two centauries. Therefore, the name was given to the plant from the name of the Centaur himself. He first prepared medicine from these herbs and handed them down to the sick.

---

Some call it *agaronion*. Others: *plectoniam*. The Punics: *Abussulum*. Others: *Junniteco*. The Greeks <note>original: "ophore," likely a corruption of "ophiouris"</note>: *meracleon*. Others call it *Cyromas*. Others: *apogitisam*. Others: *polidon*. Others: *Emercos*. The Egyptians: *Antiana*.

<margin>For pains of the spleen.</margin>

<margin>For strengthening the sight and the viscera.</margin>
A paste of the herb **Lesser Centaury**: or the <term>juice</term> recently ground and drunk with very old wine: it works most powerfully. For defects or pains of the eyes—

With the juice of the herb **Lesser Centaury**, you may anoint the eyes: it <unclear>touches</unclear> them very sharply and cleanses them; with honey added, it helps much. This is a <term>cathartic</term> <note>here meaning a cleansing or purifying agent</note>: it restores clarity to the eyes, as I have seen in this present medicine.

<margin>For flux of the belly.</margin>
A handful of the herb **Lesser Centaury** in a full measure of wine, macerated for three days, with half a pound of honey added: when well boiled, it sharply binds the belly. It most perfectly clears away the dimness of the eyes. For those with <unclear>joint pains</unclear> who are also called "slaves to Venus" <note>likely a reference to venereal disease or gout associated with luxury</note>.

Boil the herb **Lesser Centaury** in a large quantity until reduced to a fourth; and from that same water you shall give it to drink; the fourth part should be the measure of the potion. You shall give this for three days: he will be healed. For intestinal worms and larvae—

You shall give the same potion of the herb **Lesser Centaury** as described above <unclear>to be drunk</unclear>. For the effects of poison—

Give the herb **Centaury** simply wrapped in vinegar, well-crushed, to drink: soon it overcomes all poison. Also for the viscera—

Crush the roots of the herb **Lesser Centaury**: you place one <note>denarius weight</note> of it; and you shall give it to drink for three days: it is beneficial.

## Althea / Hibiscus
<note>Althea officinalis, or Marshmallow. Not to be confused with the modern garden Hibiscus.</note>

Its first cure is for gout <term>podagras</term>.
The **herb Althea**, crushed with old hog's lard <term>axungia</term> and applied, will soften the area; it is said that it will be healed. Many authors also affirm the effectiveness of this herb's extract. For all swellings <term>collectiones</term> that arise in the body: the **herb Hibiscus** cooked with fenugreek and linseed; when applied, it most perfectly disperses all swellings. For all ailments of the body's passages: the **herb Hibiscus** boiled with fenugreek and placed around the area; once applied, it heals most perfectly. For internal pains: the **herb Hibiscus** boiled alone in water; it will soothe the pain of the sinews and will be healed on a certain day. For stings or sores that arise in the fingernails: the **herb Hibiscus** [for] wounds; and when crushed with unsalted lard, applied on wood <note>likely a splint or spatula</note> and spread on a clean linen cloth, and placed over it. End.

...for open and scarred blisters.
## Isperias / Horsetail
**The name of this herb is Isperias.**
Some call it *Teucria cnon* <note>original: "Teucria cnon"</note>, others *nabatim*. Others call it *fedranon*. The Romans call it *Equiti septim* <note>original: "Equiti septim", likely Equisetum</note>. The Anthos call it *Equicena* or *nocrante*.
Its first cure is for difficult dysentery <term>uiscuridos</term>.
The expressed juice of the **herb Horsetail** <term>Equisetae</term> given to drink in a very small amount of harsh wine; it immediately restrains diarrhea. For all those who [discharge] blood through their mouth <margin>dysentery.</margin> The **herb Horsetail** <gloss>fresh</gloss>, its juice expressed and that same juice in a very small amount of firm, harsh wine given to drink: it immediately restrains the blood.
**The name of this herb is Wild Agalia.** <note>Likely Wood Betony or a similar woodland plant.</note>

## Mallow
**By the Greeks it is called *molocheagria*** <note>Wild Mallow</note>. The Romans name it Garden Mallow. The Pythagoreans call it *Introam*. Others call it *aspon*. Others: *Collistum*. And the *oivastres* call it *dudema*. The Egyptians: *lourcem*... The Italians call it Country Mallow. It grows in cultivated places. Its first cure is for kidney pain <term>nefrit dolore</term>, or for those who <insert>pass blood after [taking] mallow:</insert> take the roots of the **Mallow herb**; from there you will carefully crush one pound, boil that pound in water until it is reduced by half; give it to the thirsty person to drink, and it will immediately stop the blood. It most perfectly relieves the pain. <margin>For pain of the sinews.</margin> You shall crush the roots of the **Wild Mallow herb** with old lard. It wonderfully heals all pain of the sinews. From the roots you may make [poultices]... from the dry ones indeed with... For burning [sores] boil the **Wild Mallow herb** in oil; then rub its leaves. Let it be warmed in a mortar... <insert>To heal a painful womb</insert> Let the roots of the **Wild Mallow herb** be carefully ground... choose the **Wild Mallow herb** and pound it with unsalted lard. If Hibiscus is mixed with it at the same time, and you make a medicine from it and apply it, you will experience a marvelous thing.
**The name of this herb is Ox-tongue. Some call it Tongue.**

## Bugloss
**The Greeks call it Bugloss.** <margin>Ox-tongue in English.</margin>
The Prophets call it *Gonose lucrum*. *Astaries*. *Amc*. The Egyptians: *anthestigere*. The Romans: *lingua bubulam* <note>Ox-tongue</note>. The Lucanians: *Corraginem*. The Egyptians...
It grows everywhere in cultivated and sandy places. Against tertian or quartan fevers <note>fevers that recur every three or four days, often malaria</note>: take the liquid of the **Ox-tongue herb**, boil it with its roots in water; give that same water to be drunk carefully and it will be a good remedy. If, however, you have the course of a quartan fever, do the same and it will remedy the quartan. <margin>For an abscess.</margin> If there are **marks on the body**, you shall crush the juice of Ox-tongue with honey and bread. Apply it in the manner of a plaster <term>malagmatis</term>; it wonderfully opens abscesses and closes them by that movement. Against difficulty of breathing, give the juice of the **Ox-tongue herb** <unclear>v-ov-</unclear> with honey to be drunk by the one gasping. You shall gather it in the month of June or July.
**The name of this herb is Squill Bulb.**

# The first cure for it: for fistulas <note>A fistula is an abnormal tunnel-like passage in the body, often requiring specialized drainage.</note>.

## Take six ounces of the root of the herb Erision <note>likely Hedge Mustard or a similar cruciferous plant</note>, six ounces of starch, two cups of decoction, and three ounces of fox fat. Place these in a linen cloth and apply it; you will marvel at the good effect.

<margin>For a fractured skull</margin>

## The upper part of the herb Erision, dried and well-ground, mixed in equal weights and applied to a fractured skull, extracts the broken bones from it. Furthermore, if anything has been embedded <note>literally "put to sleep"</note> in the body, or if someone has stepped on the bones of a serpent, this same herb is effective against all poisons.

<margin>The name of this herb is Gallitricum—another one.</margin>

---

<margin>Its first cure is for scrofulous swellings <unclear>ointment</unclear>.</margin>

## The herb Gallitricum <note>likely Wild Clary or Sage</note>, diligently ground by itself and applied to the scrofulous swellings <note>strumas: swollen lymph nodes, often associated with tuberculosis</note> of young women, heals them most perfectly. For the hair:

## The herb Gallitricum is used thus to encourage growth: when well-boiled with oil, you should <unclear>anoint</unclear> the whole <unclear>crown of the head</unclear> with it. The hair is made black if you use it frequently.

## This herb Immolum is the most famous of all herbs. Homer <gloss>the Chief Physician</gloss> <unclear>mentions it</unclear>, and Mercury is assigned as its discoverer, showing it by his command for the benefit of mankind. It has a round root, and it is black and the size of an onion. For pain:

## The herb Immolum, when crushed and applied <gloss>to the joints</gloss>, heals the pain of the matter <note>referring to the "humors" or physical cause of the pain</note> most perfectly.

# Its first cure: for scrofula.
<note>Scrofula: a form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes of the neck, often called the "King's Evil."</note>

Six ounces of the root of the herb **Erysimum** <note>Hedge mustard</note>; six ounces of **Starch**; two <term>cyathi</term> <note>A Roman liquid measure, roughly 1.5 ounces</note> of **Wild Carrot**; three ounces of **fox fat**. Apply this in a linen cloth; if you do not apply it this way, it will not have a good effect.

# Against head sores
The upper part of the herb **Erysimum**, dried and well-ground. Mix it in equal weights and apply it with goat's dung. It is excellent for broken sores, or if anything in the body has become dormant <note>possibly referring to cysts or localized infections</note>, or if the feet have been bitten by a serpent without bone damage. This same herb is effective against all poisons.

<margin>Another name: the herb Cistiricum.</margin>

<margin>For scrofulous tumors.</margin>

# Its first use
The herb **Cistiricum**, when diligently ground and applied to the scrofula of young girls, heals them most effectively.

<margin>For the hair</margin>

# The herb Cistiricum, thus: by milking it <note>extracting the sap</note> and mixing it well with oil, and sprinkling it on the head; if you prick the scalp with it, the hairs will be made black, if you use it frequently.

This herb indeed directs its little flowers only toward the course of the sun; and when the sun sets, it closes them. Again, when the sun rises, it sends forth and opens its little flowers. It serves for many remedies and medicines.

# The name of this herb is Victoriola.

<margin>By the Greeks: *syfolidra*. By the English: *cannipentis*, *confonid*.</margin>

->**Some call it Confirmam.**<-
Others: *Conserbriam*. Others: *pactes*. The Italians: *Argallitum*. It grows in rich places, in fields, or among grapes.
**Its first cure: for the flux of women.**
Grind the herb <term>Conferva</term> <note>Comfrey, known for its "knitting" or "consolidating" properties</note> and at the same time let it cool down thoroughly. Give it as a drink in wine to the patient; it immediately restrains the blood. For healing burns or wounds from a fall: cook the root of the ground herb in warm ashes. Then give it to the patient three times with honey: it heals and wonderfully purges him of all <margin>languor, that is, faintness</margin>. For stomach pain: if one eats the root of the ground herb with ginger, it will be a great benefit.
**Against a heavy cough.**
Boil the root of the ground herb; grind it with a half-part of honey, 15 grams of pepper, and one <term>scruple</term> <note>a small unit of weight, about 1.3 grams</note> of <term>castoreum</term> <note>a secretion from beavers used in ancient medicine</note>. It settles a cough wonderfully. This is shown by this proof: if you put pieces of cut beef into a pot to cook, and you add this herb, you will find the meat joined back together.

# The name of the herb: Symphoniaca.

Some call it *son*. By the Greeks it is called *Daphnoydes*. Others: *Daphnes alexandrine*. Others: *Isosthyllon*. Others: *Samartacinum*. Others: *ypoglottis*. Others: *Daphnites*. Others: *Stephanon alexandrinos*. Others: *Bicinherbicam*. The Italians: *suave solum*. Others: *Laurus alexandros*. Others: *Alexandri coronam*. Others: *ypogolossum*. Others: *pecleomam*.
**Its one cure: For phlegm in the [chest].**
Take the <unclear>fresh</unclear> leaves of the herb Victoriola. Grind them to extract the juice, then mix it with honey. Give three <term>cyathi</term> <note>small cups or measures</note> to the patient while fasting. It profits him immediately.

---

<margin>Take two drachms with mutton; when cooked with beef, it will cohere into one. The name of this herb: Asterion.</margin>

The Romans call this herb *Inquinaria*. For it grows among rocks and rough places. This same herb shines at night like a star, and those who see it and do not know it say they see a phantom, and are filled with fear and tremble. It is mostly found by the shepherds of flocks.
**Its one cure: Against the Falling Sickness <note>Epilepsy</note>.**
If you give the herb Asterion to a person with the falling sickness to eat while the moon is waning, and while the sun is in the sign of Virgo, and he has the same herb hanging from his neck: he will be cured. We have tested this frequently; do not doubt it.

---

<margin>The herb *grypophylla*.</margin>

# Name of the herb: Leporis pes (Hare's Foot). <insert>Sananicula</insert>
The Africans call this herb *Vemum*. The Romans call it the herb *grypophylla*.

**Its first cure:**
**To loosen the belly.**
The herb Hare's Foot is dried and reduced to a powder; it is given in a drink with wine. If, however, the patient has a fever, you shall give it in warm water; I know it loosens the belly.
**Against the flow of women.**
Hang a small sprig of this herb around the neck of the patient: the flow immediately stops.
**The name of this herb: Diptanus (Dittany).**
The Greeks call this herb *Arthemidisocre*. Others: *Bubulonitum*. Others: *Epimeron*. Others: *Cleacows*. Others: *Bercadion*. Others: *Anclion*. The Italians: *Tusillam rusticam*. The Egyptians: *Amenypsum*. Others: *Hyplacum*. The Romans, however: *Poleum Agreste*. For it grows on the island of Crete, on Mount Ida.

## Against venomous wounds.
The herb Dittany, also known as agrimony or ambrosia: apply their juice with butter to the wounds. In all things, Dittany will be of use.

By the Greeks it is called *Pentorobon*. Or others call it *Aglaophotis*; others *Peonia*. <margin>For a fistulous cancerous ulcer <note>original: "fistylam cancrenam Dictuolam"</note>.</margin>
By some, it is called *militaris achillea* <note>Soldier's Yarrow</note>. By others, indeed, it is called *supercilium veneris* <note>Venus's Eyebrow</note>. Others call it *Cercum siluacorum*.

### Against all wounds.

<margin>Achillea <note>Yarrow</note>.</margin>
It is said that Achilles discovered this herb; since he himself was most skilled in the art of the blade, when he was wounded, he would apply it and heal himself. Hence it is called *achillea*. Concerning this herb: it is called *acilefon* for healing.

### Against toothaches.

Crush the herb *millefolium* <note>Yarrow/Milfoil</note>. If it is dry: give it to the patient to drink with vinegar; immediately the urine is released and provoked. For indeed, it takes away the pain of the teeth at once.

<margin>Rue.</margin>
### Name of the herb: Rue.

By the Greeks, it is called <term>peganon</term> <note>Greek: πήγανον</note>. This herb has ten virtues. The first cures <term>ephinf</term> <note>likely a corruption of 'epiphora' or a specific nasal ailment</note>: for blood flowing forth from the nostrils.

---

### -> Rue. <-

The herb Rue, frequently smelled: wonderfully restrains blood flowing from the nostrils.

### Against inflammations.

Warm a little of the herb Rue of the East and place it in a potion with honey. If taken in this way: it is most powerfully effective.

### The herb Rue for stomach pains.

Crush the seed with sulfur. If eaten in this way: they will experience a healthy medicine.

### The herb Rue for pains of the groin.

Green Rue: boil it with oil. Afterward, let it be mixed into an ointment, from which you may make a wax-salve. Apply it with a linen cloth to the groin: it heals perfectly.

### For watery eyes <note>original: "epiforas oculorum"</note>.

The herb Rue well-crushed with polenta <note>barley meal</note> and applied: soothes watery eyes. For also its root, when crushed and smeared over the eyes: heals watery eyes most excellently.

---

### For dimness of the eyes.

Let the herb Rue be gathered while the morning dew is biting <note>fresh at dawn</note>. Let it be placed in a clean vessel. And with that same herb and roses: frequently anoint the eyes. The dimness of the eyes is removed.

### For Carbuncles <note>skin boils</note>.

Place the herb Rue mixed with vinegar and wax over the carbuncles. They will be healed.

### For wounds.

The leaves of the herb Rue with honey and pitch, most diligently <unclear>crushed</unclear> and applied: heals wounds most perfectly. Boil a bundle of the herb Rue and an ounce of rose oil: with which you should prick <note>or perhaps 'anoint'</note> the patient.

### Against the bite of a mad dog.

Crush two denarii-weight <note>a Roman measurement</note> of a potion of the herb Rue and give it to be drunk with wine: it is very beneficial for the bite and resists all poison.

---

### Herb for curing swellings.

Rue crushed with vinegar and oil: and you should prick the swellings; it heals them most excellently. Thus also it is sustained from head to foot. [If treated] with iron and wine: but if it should happen that it bleeds: he will be deserving [of recovery].

### For sharpness of the eyes.

Let one frequently eat the crushed leaves of the herb Rue: or give the same green herb to be drunk with wine. For it will sharpen the sight of the eyes.

### For the flow of a woman <note>menstruation or hemorrhage</note>.

Encircle the herb Rue with gold and silver. You shall bind it [to her] during the waning moon.

### For the flow of blood.

The herb Rue <unclear>with a coin</unclear> heals. It wonderfully binds the blood.

### <note>Remedy for head pain</note>

The herb Rue given to drink with wine and oil: or anoint the head with vinegar or rose oil. It heals the ache of the head.

<page-num>91</page-num>

The herb **Dwarf Elder** <note>original: "egentastru" / "ebulo"; botanical name: *Sambucus ebulus*</note>. Mix the fruit with one small cup <term>cyathus: a liquid measure roughly equal to 1.5 ounces</term> of harsh wine. Pour it into the ear when it is empty <note>likely meaning an ear canal free of obstruction</note>. The pain is taken away immediately. And if worms have been born there, it will kill them by its own power. For elephantiasis <note>a term used in antiquity for various severe skin diseases or leprosy</note>:

Give the herb Dwarf Elder along with its own leaves to be eaten. It is very beneficial for elephantiasis; it cures them gently and cleanses them thoroughly.

<gloss>The names of this herb: *Ebulo*, that is, Elder.</gloss>

For the bite of a snake, give the herb Dwarf Elder, which when dried with wax... say the name three times <note>a common ritualistic or "magical" element in medieval medicine</note>.

The herb Dwarf Elder for healing those with dropsy <term>ydropicus: edema or swelling caused by fluid retention</term>. Take the juice of the root; then give three spoonfuls with a half-pint <term>emina: approximately 0.27 liters</term> of wine as a drink to the person with dropsy. Indeed, it will be most powerfully beneficial, for all the worst fluids are drawn out through the cloths <note>possibly referring to poultices or the purging effect</note>.

For pain of the spleen, use the dried root of the herb Dwarf Elder. Reduced into a very soft powder and mixed into wine in whatever cups you use; then let the person with the spleen ailment drink one spoonful immediately in the light of the house. Let him always have Dwarf Elder with him; it is a most healthy remedy for the liver.

<margin>Pennyroyal Pennyroyal</margin>

By the Greeks it is called *Canutum*. Others call it *chechos* or *afiadnes*. Others: *Cameacte*. Others: *adhorechoc*. Others: *Ebuon*. The Danes: *Elman*. The Italians call it *Ebulum*.

The herb Dwarf Elder against kidney stones: given as a drink in wine with the leaves of burdock <note>original: "arcium"</note>, it expels the stones.

Against the spleen.

Boil the root of the herb Dwarf Elder in water until it is reduced by a third. Give it to drink; you will marvel at its good effect.

<header>The Herb Pennyroyal</header>

...red or purple. Both have the same power. This herb, however, has a useful and wonderful virtue in itself, especially when they bloom with colors and the leaves dry out. Its first cure: let the herb Pennyroyal <note>original: "puleij" / "pulegium"; botanical name: *Mentha pulegium*</note> be dried by the sun.

Carry the herb Pennyroyal with you...

<gloss>Against the pain of the womb in infants</gloss>

Let the herb Pennyroyal be carefully ground and tempered with water; and when you place it with cumin over the infant's navel, the infant will immediately become healthy.

Against nausea of the stomach.

Give the herb Pennyroyal crushed with water or vinegar as a drink; it gently soothes the nausea of the stomach.

Against itching of the belly.

The herb Pennyroyal, when chewed and fixed upon the navel, conquers the pain immediately.

For "murenas" <note>likely referring to "murena" or "murena-like" sores, or perhaps a type of creeping ulcer</note>.

Seek the herb Pennyroyal. From it, take firm young branches and wrap them in wool. If you give it to the sufferer before the onset <note>of a fever or fit</note>, and he wears a crown of it on his head, he will not feel the pain of the head.

If a woman is in great labor with a child.

Take three fresh branches of the herb Pennyroyal which you have smelled carefully; give it to drink with old wine. If it is a "narca" <note>possibly a numbness or paralysis</note> of the fourth part, it casts out the dead infant without danger.

Lest you be seasick in a ship.

Keep the herb Pennyroyal with vinegar and oil, and with it frequently touch the nostrils.

If a woman is not sufficiently cleansed <note>referring to postpartum discharge or menstruation</note>.

Give the seeds of the herb Pennyroyal in two cups of oxymel <term>oxymel: a medicinal drink made of honey and vinegar</term> for her to drink.

If the afterbirth remains after giving birth. <gloss>For spots on the face from heat</gloss>

Two cups of the herb Pennyroyal well-boiled. If drunk in the usual way, it immediately protects the skin and heals redness and drives out the heat.

If anyone has a cough or chest pain. Put two cups in vinegar. Let him drink it and he will be freed.

If a woman...

The herb Pennyroyal, when ground and reduced to powder and wrapped in wool, if applied below, it will be wonderfully healed.

For stomach pain without inflammation, it will be wonderfully healed.

For bites...

The herb Pennyroyal restrains the pain. When carefully prepared by itself with hot water and given as a drink, it will be wonderfully healed.

For the movement <note>likely of the bowels or bile</note>...

Take the joints of the herb Pennyroyal. Put a quarter-measure in oxymel; then give two cups to the patient to drink while fasting; it will cast out the bile.

Against pain of the spleen.

Carefully boil the herb Pennyroyal in vinegar for those with spleen ailments. If you drink it, you will feel a great benefit quickly.

Grind the herb Pennyroyal with equal weights of pepper; if you are in pain in the bath, rub it on and it heals in that manner.

# Tormentil / Hog’s Fennel / Elecampane

<margin>English ---</margin>
The name of this herb is Tormentil <note>original: *Heptafilon*, meaning "seven-leaved"</note>.
The Greeks call it *Calannos*.
The Italians call it *Heptana* <note>Seven-fold</note> among our people.
The first cure for it: Against tertian or daily fevers. The herb *septana*, reduced to a powder and drunk in warm water at the onset of the fever, heals wonderfully.
**H**erb <note>The manuscript uses "B" for "H"</note> for snake bites: The juice of the mountain *septana* herb, pressed and given to drink with wine, or even the crushed leaves applied to the wound, heals it.

<margin>The name of this herb is Hog’s Fennel.</margin>
The Greeks indeed call it *Peucedanum*. Others call it *Simonagron*. The prophet Agathodaemon <note>A legendary figure in Greco-Egyptian alchemy and medicine</note> calls it so. The Italians call it *Pia veridellum*. Others call it *Agnopilon*. The Gauls call it *Satanaria*.
The first cure for it: For driving away snakes.
**H**erb Hog’s Fennel: When burned, the snakes hate its vapor.
**H**erb Hog’s Fennel: For snake bites. Give it to those who have been bitten, and also apply it to the wound with [unclear] and [unclear] <note>original: *necomca y feyo armis fcas* - likely corrupted Latin for a poultice mixture</note>; it will heal.
**H**erb Hog’s Fennel: For those suffering from frenzy <note>freneticos: a term for delirium or mental agitation</note>. For when poured over the heads of the frenzied with vinegar, it does the greatest good.

<margin>The name of this herb is Elecampane.</margin>
<margin>...with Centaury... <note>Fragmentary marginalia</note></margin>
Which some call *cauria* or *clenam*. The Italians call it *Inula campana*.
The first cure for it: For pains of the belly.
**H**erb Elecampane: Grind the seeds of *sapsi* <note>possibly wild thyme or savory</note>, asparagus, and fennel roots in wine; give this potion to be drunk, and it is effectively beneficial.
Against loose teeth:
**H**erb Elecampane: They eat it, and it is given as a cure for moving teeth; immediately the teeth are made firm.

# Hound’s-tongue / Saxifrage / Black Ivy

The name of this herb is Hound’s-tongue <note>original: *Cynoglossa*</note>.
It affects those who are envious <note>A cryptic medieval reference to the plant's folklore</note>.

The Greeks call it *Cynoglossa*. Others call it *fragua*. Others *Emunion*. Others *Splenion*. Others *Catullion*. Others *loetunbos*. The Egyptians call it *pyrgis*. The Dacians call it *Cent*. Others *uicer Lam*. Others *Subluum*. Others *Macedonian tongue*. The Italians call it *Canine tongue* <note>Linguam caninam</note>.
The first cure for it:
**H**erb Hound’s-tongue: For ear pain. The juice of the *glossa* warmed with raisin wine and oil and dripped into the ears wonderfully takes away the pain of the ears.
**H**erb Hound’s-tongue: Against quartan fevers: Let the patient take three leaves and give them as a drink with water; they will be quickly freed.
For snake bites:
**H**erb Hound’s-tongue: Crushed and taken in wine, it benefits the bites of snakes.

The name of this herb is Saxifrage <note>literally "Stone-breaker"</note>.
<margin>Stone.</margin>
<margin>Saxifrage</margin>
Some call it *Scolopendria*. Others *Scolionof*. Others *Vuatcanam*. The Egyptians call it *Peperem*. Others *dprocom*. Others name it *Lampocom*. For it grows in mountains and rocky places.
<margin>for the stone</margin>
**T**he only cure for it: For expelling kidney stones <note>calculos</note>. You shall give this herb, well-crushed, to the sufferer of stones to drink in wine. If, however, a fever has been stirred up, give it with warm water. Its effect is so immediate, as handed down by experience, that on the same day it crushes and leads to the ejection of the stones, bringing the patient to health.

The name of this herb is Black Ivy.
<margin>black ivy</margin>

### Cyssonmelleia / Ivy

By some, this herb is called: **Cyssonmelleia** <note>original: "Cyſſonmelleia", likely a corruption of a Greek term for a type of ivy</note>. Others indeed call it: **Butlusserion**. The **Dacians** <note>People from the Roman province of Dacia, modern-day Romania</note> call it: **Arpropa**. The Italians, however, call it: **Black Ivy**. Its first cure is for:

**B**y using the berries of the Ivy herb to expel stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>. Crush seven or nine berries and give them in water to the one suffering from stones to drink; it heals wonderfully and quickly. For the head:

**B**y mixing the juice of the Ivy herb for a wound with rose oil in wine; then anoint the head, and the pain of the head is soothed. Against pain of the spleen:

**B**y giving five berries of Black Ivy in wine for several days to those suffering from spleen ailments, if they are without fever. If, however, they are feverish, give them in warm water; this will be of the greatest benefit. Do this for five days. Against the bite of a venomous spider <note>original: "spalangioni", referring to the phalangiid spider</note>:

**B**y crushing the roots of the Ivy herb and giving the expressed juice to drink; it is wonderfully beneficial for the bite of venomous spiders. For foul-smelling nostrils:

**B**y pouring the very clearly strained juice of the Ivy herb into foul-smelling nostrils; it corrects the state wonderfully. For:

**B**y dropping the very clearly strained juice of the Ivy herb into the ears with wine for ear pain; it likewise cures the pain. Against headache from the sun:

**B**y smearing the very soft leaves and branches of the Ivy herb with vinegar and rose oil against all pain of the forehead; the pain departs immediately. The following herb:

The **Greeks** call this herb: **Serpillum** <note>Wild Thyme</note>. Others: **Serpillon**. Others, the Egyptians: **Merum**. The Italians: **Serpilli**. Some indeed call it: **Rustic Thyme**.

---

### Serpillum (Wild Thyme)

**I**ts first cure is for headache.
**B**y rubbing the head with dry Thyme herb mixed with oil and salt, and reducing it to a powder to prick <note>or stimulate</note> the head; it will heal quickly. For healing a burn:

**B**y taking the herb Thyme and alkanet <note>original: "anchuse"</note> with three ounces each of silver dross <note>litharge</note> and roses; crush them together in a mortar. Then take a little wax with bear fat and deer tallow; boil it down into one pound. Strain it carefully and use it.

### Absinthium (Wormwood)

**T**he herb Wormwood is a bitter plant; it grows in cultivated and mountainous places. For the chest, fumigate with Wormwood... <note>The Latin here is fragmented and contains obscure medicinal terms</note> ...present it as a drink. For removing worms:

**B**y boiling the herb Wormwood in water, soaking a cloth in it, and applying it; it removes worms. If, however, the body is tender, apply it with honey. Against worms:

**B**y crushing the herb Wormwood, horehound, and lupine in equal weights, and boiling them in honeyed water or in harsh wine; applied to the navel, it kills worms immediately. For the groin, or troubles of the groin:

**B**y taking a sprig of the herb Wormwood... it flees the pain of the groin and soothes it wonderfully. The name of this herb is also called Salma.

<page-num>39</page-num>

The herb Wormwood <note>original: "absinthia"</note> grows in cultivated and mountainous places. For a chest <note>The scribe likely meant "pectus" (chest) or "potio" (drink)</note> affected by poisonous mushrooms, grind the wormwood diligently; then express the juice and give it as a drink to the patient. Indeed, that draft is a saved life <note>original: "peculū Servatū"</note> for one who has escaped poisoning after being overcome by mushrooms.

**To remove bruises.**

The herb Wormwood, boiled in water, applied to a cloth and placed on the area, removes bruises. If, however, the body is tender, apply it with honey.

**Against intestinal worms.**

Grind the herb Wormwood, Horehound <note>original: "marrubiū"</note>, and Lupin in equal weights; boil them in plenty of water or in a harsh wine. Placed upon the navel, it kills the worms immediately.

**For troubles of the groin or chafing.**

Take a sprig of the herb Wormwood and carry it with you in your belt; it drives away the pain of the groin or chafing <note>original: "intertriginū"</note> and heals them wonderfully.

# The name of the herb: Coriander.
->That is, Coriandre.<-

**Its first cure: for intestinal worms.**

Boil the herb Coriander down to its essence in oil; then anoint the head. It is wonderfully beneficial.

**So that a woman may give birth quickly.**

Take eleven or thirteen grains of the herb Coriander and tie them in a clean linen cloth with a thread from the loom. Let a boy or a girl hold this against the left thigh near the groin; as soon as the birth has taken place, it will be a remedy. # Folio 40v

<margin>109</margin>
The herb **Burnallis** <note>likely Self-heal or a similar wound-herb</note> produces fruit twice in a completed year. You shall give the juice of this herb to a youth to drink in two measures <note>original: "cleand duo," likely a corruption of a specific liquid measure</note>; he will be quickly healed. **For warts:**
The milk of the herb **Crauallis** <note>possibly a variant of Celandine</note> with the flower of the herb **Begacion** <note>possibly Betony</note>, if you place it upon the warts, they will be healed on the third day. **For healing fistulas:**
The juice of the herb **Burnallis** mixed with wine, [applied] over the <term>fistula</term> <note>an abnormal tunnel-like sore in the body</note>, will be healed wonderfully. **For lepers:**
The flower of the herb **Burnallis** with resin of the <term>cocco</term> <note>likely kermes or a specific berry resin</note> shall be carefully preserved; then let him smear the lepers with it. It works and is beneficial; it has been proven by experience. **This is the herb Wild Thistle.**

# **H**erb: Wild Chicory <note>original: "Sicuris agria"</note> should be gathered in the month of July, when the herbs are growing, on the third day of the moon, so that it is effective for all things.

### The Conjuration of the herb itself:

> **O** Earth, I ask for those things which are written at the beginning of this book. <note>original: "O ea tã tellus..." refers to a standard ritual invocation to the personified Earth found in medieval herbals.</note>

### This is the description of the herb itself.

**H**owever, this herb, Basilica <note>The "Royal" herb, often associated with Basil or similar protective plants</note>, grows in places where the Basilisk serpent has been. For there is not just one kind of this serpent, but there are three kinds. The first is called the *Holochristus* <note>original: "Olocristus", likely a corruption of a Greek term for "all-golden"</note>, which has a golden crest on its head. The third is the Sanguineous <note>Bloody</note>, similar to a rhinoceros, having a golden head. This Basilica herb keeps all these serpents under its power; if anyone has it with them, whatever serpent they see will not be able to harm them. The *Holochristus*, which is called "golden," breathes upon whatever it sees and it perishes. The Starry one, which is the *Chrysocephalus* <note>Golden-headed</note>, stands still: that which it sees withers and dies. The third, indeed, which is the Sanguineous and *Chrysostatus*, whatever it sees or strikes will dissolve, so that only dry ground remains. Truly, this royal herb overcomes all the deaths caused by these serpents. For whatever man has it with him will be safe from every kind of serpent. Furthermore, this Basilica herb is such: its root, as if seen, has a golden milk like the juice of Celandine. It has a golden-seeded flower, and the leaves are like those of a radish, but longer and narrower than the leaves of a cup, sprinkled with black drops.

<column-break/>

...it: you purify it thus. And let him circumscribe it with gold, silver, ivory, a rhinoceros tooth, and a bull's horn, and place honeyed fruits in the footprint. **The experiences of the Narrow Lozenges are shown here.** <note>original: "Angustidum pastilloy", likely referring to medicinal lozenges or troches.</note>

**I**t is said that he who used the narrow [lozenge] at the command [of the physician]—which I say brings strength to the whole body because the firmness of honey is in it—whoever uses it will not suffer pain of the stomach, nor of the liver, nor of the gallbladder, nor of the spleen, nor of the kidneys, nor of the heart; nor will there be sciatica, nor will his side ache, nor his lungs, nor will he suffer any spasm, nor infirmity, nor a chill, nor pain of the neck. There will be no dysentery, nor will he be dropsical from its composition, nor suffer shortness of breath. It works for all pains and precious humors. It works for pain of the chest; it is powerful against gluttony and against mortal dangers. It profits every danger of the body. And if you suspect poisons, if one has used this lozenge, he will be immediately freed and it expels all evils. Furthermore, this is how the composition of this kind of lozenge is received. **Its composition:**

Of the highest seed: one ounce. Of gold: [taken]. Of wine: 1 ounce. Of *ririntrum*: [1 ounce]. Of *einbacum*: 1 ounce. Of *rny*: 1 ounce. Of *peleis*: 1 ounce. Of *riganti*: 1 ounce. Of white stone: 1 ounce. Of *brocami*: half an ounce. Of black *sperti*: 1 ounce. Of alum: 1 ounce. Of *reri* seed: one ounce. Of *furne*: [1 ounce]. Of *arbe iouis* <note>Jupiter's herb</note>: 1 ounce. Of celery seed: 1 ounce. Of *anti stachet*: 1 ounce. In the root of this and of Capsella: 6 half-ounces.

If indeed it is cooked from the bark of the root itself, let it be done until it reaches the thickness of honey; let it be put away for medicinal use. Let the roots be saved; they are profitable for many uses.

# The name of this herb: Mandrake.

<margin>mandrake</margin> <margin>male</margin>

There are two kinds of this herb: the male and the female. The male has whiter and larger leaves, and bears fruit the size of Matian apples <note>original: "mali matiana" - a variety of apple famous in Rome, named after Gaius Matius</note>. Both, however, possess the same primary power for treating inflammations of the eyes.

**T**he fresh leaves of the mandrake herb, when very carefully ground with pearl barley and applied to the eyes, are most effective for wounds and burning of the eyes. They dissolve and disperse all swellings and all hard growths. Against marks on the body:

**T**ake fresh leaves of the mandrake herb; gently rub the marks on the body with them. It clears them away without causing any ulceration. If you preserve these same leaves <gloss>in</gloss> <gloss>white</gloss> <gloss>salt</gloss> <gloss>the same</gloss> among white willow branches, they furthermore retain their healing power for all these uses.

### For the Holy Fire.
<note>"Holy Fire" or "Ignis Sacer" usually referred to Erysipelas or similar skin inflammations in medieval medicine.</note>

**T**he root of the mandrake herb, ground with vinegar and smeared on, perfectly cures the holy fire.

### Against the bite of a serpent.

**T**he root of the mandrake herb, applied with oil or with honey, soothes the bite of a serpent.

### Against scrofula.
<note>original: "chyriadas" - referring to swollen lymph nodes, often associated with tuberculosis.</note>

**T**he leaves of the mandrake herb, ground with water and applied, disperse scrofulous swellings.

### For pain in the joints.

**T**he leaves of the mandrake herb, ground with pearl barley and applied, pacify the pain of the joints.

### For sleep and bodily
ills. If they are smelled, so that they are hated <note>likely meaning the scent is so strong it causes an aversion</note>, they cause sleep and take away the voice from the body. Also, the juice expressed from the bark of its root is cooked down in an earthen vessel or in the fiery sun, being stirred constantly until it reaches the thickness of honey. Once prepared, it should be stored for medical use. The dried roots, if reserved, are beneficial for many uses.

---

The name of this herb is called Ground Pine.
<note>original: "Camepitu" from the Greek "Chamaipitys".</note>

**S**ome call it *Oboizelos*. Others indeed call it *Olochton*. For the prophets call it *Emaatanas*. Others: *Dioniagrif*. Others: *Ibigam*. Others: Black *Cypeffim*. Others say *Rapum*. Others: *Spicula*. The Egyptians: *Mammaleam*. The Dacians: *Henrenepfam*. The Greeks indeed call it *Camepitum* <note>original: "Camepitum", likely Ground Pine (Ajuga chamaepitys)</note>.
