**The herb Ground Pine**
**T**he herb Ground Pine, reduced into a very soft powder; if however the wound is fresh, let it be placed into the wounds just as it is.

**The herb Ground Pine**

### concerning medicinal masculine herbs

### which some call a badger <note>original: "melem"</note>. Others indeed
### call it a "Taxo" <note>The Latin "taxo" is the root for the modern Italian "tasso" and French "tesson" for badger</note>. Sent by the King of the Egyptians to

**T**he King of the Egyptians to Octavian Augustus,
greetings. By many examples I have experienced
your victory and wisdom. For I judge that there has never
fallen into hands a man of such great utility, whom
we received from Asclepius <note>The Greek god of medicine</note> and who, with
the greatest cares and my edicts,

-> we have judged to be a worthy <-
-> reward: a small four-legged beast <-

which you call a "taxo," and which
they also call an "egelem" <note>likely a variant of "meles", badger</note>. And this is so that no one
may be able to harm you or yours with evil deeds, nor with
failings, such as happened to my army in arms.
When you catch it, pull out its teeth while it is alive,
those which are large; and when you have pulled them out,
you shall say these prayers:

> Bucabat: torthon: hyidam: cernathlini: con-
> bon. boronardisontas. annorden. <note>These are "voces magicae" or non-lexical magical words intended for ritual power</note>

You shall say these words nine times. And these teeth,
pierce them and bind them in gold to your arm,
and with their powers clinging to you as if to your body,
neither an unlucky star, nor incantations, nor time will harm you,
nor pestilence, nor evil encounters
will do harm. Or if any evil occurs, the
tooth breaks immediately. Its right foot—
when you have enchanted it with these words—in whatever
contention or fight you are in, you will be superior to all,
as long as you have its foot with you. Also its fat:
enchant it with these words, and whomever you anoint
with it when they are sick, it will immediately draw out the fever.
Provided, however, that the person is still vital; for
delay does not prolong time. If the disease or cause is greater,
it quickly recedes. Boil its brain in three sextarii <note>A Roman measure, roughly half a liter</note>
of oil; let it boil down until a third
part remains, and strain the rest.
And if anyone is vexed by pain of the head or feet,
let him treat the head with it, or...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal-based remedies, specifically focusing on the medicinal properties of the stag (cervus), following previous sections on other forest creatures.</meta>

# On the Stag

<note>original: "De cervo"</note>

Its primary use is for drying out all the worst humors <note>In medieval medicine, "humors" were the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which needed to be balanced for health.</note>.

**D**eer horn has the power to dry out all humors; those suffering from them use it in poultices.

### Against teeth that are loose.

**B**urn deer horn and reduce it to a powder. Rub the teeth that are moving with that ash; it strengthens them most perfectly.

### For the royal disease <note>The "royal disease" (morbus regius) is a historical term for jaundice, so named because it was thought to be cured by the touch of a king, or perhaps because of the golden color of the skin.</note>.

**G**ive the patient one drachm <note>A drachm is a small unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams.</note> of powdered deer horn with one cup of wine and two cups of water. You will be amazed by the good effect.

### It is used in the same way for loosening of the belly <note>diarrhea</note>.

### For flows of blood.

**B**urnt deer horn, ground very finely and given as a drink, restrains the blood.

### Against intestinal worms.

**D**eer horn, either burnt or filed down, and given to the patient to drink in the same way with wine or in warm water, expels worms from the body.

---

### To put serpents to flight.

**B**urn deer horn; immediately all kinds of serpents will flee.

### For suffocations of the womb <note>A historical diagnosis for various gynecological distresses, often associated with hysteria or displacement of the uterus.</note>.

**G**ive burnt deer horn to the woman to smell; she will be healed quickly.

<gloss>Against skin eruptions</gloss>
**B**urn deer horn and take its ash. Mix it with squill vinegar <note>Vinegar infused with the sea onion (squill), used for its medicinal properties.</note> and smear it only on the place where the eruptions are; it smooths them perfectly.

### For chafing.

**T**ake four drachms of deer horn, two drachms of silver dross <note>litharge</note>, and two obols <note>A very small weight, about 0.7 grams.</note> of split alum. From these, make a salve with myrtle oil, and use it when it is prepared.

### For the groin.

**I**f you have a deer's kneecap <note>patella</note> with you, your groin will not swell; and if there is an old swelling, it quickly recedes.

### For arousing sexual desire.

**C**arry a certain part of a dried deer's testicle and drink it; it excites sexual desire. It happens thus: they do it with will.

### Against the bites of serpents.

**I**f you have the dried genitals of a deer, and if you take one drachm of it and place it in a soft-boiled egg and drink it all, every bite of a serpent will become harmless to you.

### So that no serpent approaches a person.

**I**f you wear deer skin, no serpent will approach you.

### To bind the belly.

**I**f you drink from the nature <note>likely referring to the genitals or a specific organ</note> of a deer with blood-red or harsh wine, it binds the belly wonderfully. Likewise.

It reflects the medieval "materia medica" tradition where animal parts were believed to possess specific drying or protective qualities.</summary>

<header>Concerning the Stag</header>

### Its primary cure: for drying out all the worst humors.
**D**eer horn has the power to dry out all humors <note>In medieval medicine, "humors" were the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance determined health.</note>. Those suffering from these should use it in eye-salves <term>collyriis: medicinal lotions or ointments for the eyes</term>.

### Against teeth that are loose.
**B**urn the deer horn and reduce it to a powder. Rub that ash onto the teeth that are moving; it strengthens them most perfectly.

### For the Royal Disease <note>original: "morbum regium" - a historical term for Jaundice, so named because it was thought to require the costly remedies of a king.</note>
**G**ive the patient one drachm <note>A small unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams.</note> of powdered deer horn with one cup of wine and two cups of water to drink. You will marvel at the good effect.

### For looseness of the belly; by the same reasoning it binds the belly.
**D**eer horn, burned and ground very finely and given as a drink, restrains the flow of blood.

### Against intestinal worms.
**D**eer horn, either chalky <note>likely referring to the calcined, burnt state</note> or filed down, and given in the same way to the patient as a drink with wine or in warm water, drives intestinal worms out.

---

### For putting serpents to flight.
**B**urn deer horn; immediately you will drive away all kinds of serpents.

### For suffocation of the womb.
**O**ffer burned deer horn to a woman to be smelled; it will heal her quickly. <note>This refers to "hysterical suffocation," a historical diagnosis where the womb was believed to move upward in the body, causing respiratory distress; strong smells were used to "drive" it back down.</note>

<margin>Against skin eruptions <note>original: "impetigines" - scaly or itchy skin rashes.</note></margin>
**B**urn deer horn and take its ash. Mix it with vinegar of squill <note>A medicinal vinegar made from the sea onion.</note> and smear it only on the place where the eruptions are; it heals most perfectly.

### For chafing.
**T**ake four drachms of deer horn, two drachms of silver dross <term>spumē argenti: litharge or lead oxide, a common ingredient in ancient ointments</term>, and two obols <note>A very small weight</note> of squill ointment. From these, make a paste with myrtle oil and use it on the affected area.

### For the groin.
**I**f you keep a deer’s kneecap <note>original: "patellam"</note> with you, your groin will not swell; and if there is an old swelling, it quickly recedes.

### For performing sexual intercourse.
**Y**ou shall carry some part of a dried deer’s testicle, and she shall carry it as well; it stimulates intercourse. This will happen if it is done with desire.

### Against the bites of serpents.
**I**f you have the dried "nature" <note>original: "naturam" - likely referring to the male genitalia or a specific organ</note> of a deer, and you scrape one drachm from it and put it into sippable wine and drink it all, every bite of a serpent will become harmless to you.

### So that no serpent may approach a person.
**I**f you are clothed in a deer skin, no serpent will come near you.

### For binding the belly.
**I**f you drink from the "nature" of a deer with blood-red or harsh wine, it binds the belly marvelously. **Likewise**

<meta>This page continues a series of pharmacological remedies derived from animals, specifically concluding the section on the deer (cervus) and beginning the section on the hare (lepus). The text follows the tradition of Sextus Placitus's "Liber Medicinae ex Animalibus."</meta>

# For the same [ailment], from experience.

**S** If you take the nature of a deer <note>likely referring to deer musk or a specific glandular secretion</note> in the size of a nut, or in a lentil, or in a powder, and the patient eats it, the belly is immediately constricted <note>i.e., a remedy for diarrhea</note>.

## To put all serpents to flight.

**P** If you anoint your whole body with the marrow of a deer, no serpent will harm you.

## For conception, so that a woman may conceive.

**L** A stone which is found in the womb or in the stomach of a deer is a phylactery <note>an amulet or protective charm</note>; for a pregnant woman, it is effective for a very easy birth. Women should carry it with them, because reason suggests that since that animal is very swift, it will neither cause a miscarriage nor allow one to happen. For the same reason, small bones are found either in the heart or in the womb, which provide the same benefit.

## For a woman, so that she does not conceive.

**I** The same small bone, when found and suspended from the arm, prevents a woman from conceiving.

## For those with gas.

**C** Deer marrow melted and poured into <unclear>tylana</unclear> will be a great remedy for those suffering from gas <term>carminosis: those suffering from wind or flatulence</term>.

## To put serpents to flight.

**O** If you make a tallow from the oil of deer marrow and use it as a fumigant, it puts serpents to flight; or simply keep it near you.

# Here begins the section on the hare.

**L** Take the brain of a hare—**this is the first cure**—and give it in wine to those who are drowsy <note>original: "somnolentos"</note> to drink; it cures them most perfectly.

## For pain of the eyes.

<margin>lonytb <note>This marginalia appears to be a shorthand or reference mark, possibly a shelf mark or a corrupted Greek term.</note></margin> **L** The lung of a hare, placed underneath and bound [to the site], soothes the pain of the eyes.

## For chilblains and feet injured by footwear.

**L** The lung of a hare, whether drunk or eaten, or applied to the feet, brings a remedy.

**L** From a living hare

## For quartan fevers.

[If] taken from the ear and suspended from the neck or arm of one suffering from quartan fever <note>a fever that recurs every four days, often associated with malaria</note>, it cures them most perfectly.

## For the "places" of women <note>referring to the uterus or vaginal tract</note>.

**L** Give a third part of a dried hare's heart; grind it with manna of frankincense. Give it to the patient to drink in white wine for seven days; it cures [her].

## Against the falling sickness <note>epilepsy</note>.

**L** Take a dried hare's heart and grind it down; give it to those who frequently fall <note>epileptics</note> to drink, up to the thirtieth day. However, while the moon is waxing, the potion ought to be seasoned with Attic wine; he will be healed miraculously.

## For a woman's discharge, and for her who produces blood, and for those who labor after childbirth.

**L** A scraped hare's heart given to drink with warm water and Samian earth <note>a medicinal clay from Samos</note> strongly restrains the blood.

## For women...

**L** A dried hare's heart given as a drink, as above, to women suffering from a flux and those who discharge blood, is a greatest remedy.

## Against dimness of the eyes.

**F** The gall of a hare helps the eyes; it brings clarity to the eyes and dispels imminent dimness.

## For those who are troubled by vertigo.

**L** Mix the gall of a hare with three drachms <term>drachma: a unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams</term> of weasel <unclear>textis</unclear>, one drachm of castor <note>castoreum, a secretion from beavers</note>, and four drachms of myrtle. All these are gathered in the best vinegar, and from there you shall give one drachm to drink with vinegar, honey-water, honey, or raisin wine. They will be healed.

## For those with stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>.

**For women, so that they may conceive.**

# Here begins the section on the Fox.

The first medicine of the same: lest the parts of women be suffocated by the womb <note>original: "a matrice suffocantur." This refers to "uterine suffocation" or "hysterical suffocation," a common medieval belief that the womb could move within the body and cause respiratory distress.</note>.

**T**he joints of a fox boiled in old oil together with bitumen <note>a naturally occurring petroleum or tar-like substance</note> and applied as a pessary <note>original: "propesso supposita." A medicinal plug inserted into the vagina.</note> heals the jaundice of women—that is, the natural ailments of those parts.

**Against hair loss.**

**T**hat which is written above, if the head is washed with it, it heals <term>alopecia</term> <note>the medical term for patchy hair loss</note>.

**For ear pain.**

**A**s it seems stated above, if dripped into the ears, it most perfectly takes away the earache.

**For pain of the spleen.**

**A** fox’s lung buried in hot ashes so that it may be cooked, and given in a drink to one fasting, takes away the <term>splenetic</term> <note>original: "ieconitritis." Likely a corruption of a Greek term for spleen or liver inflammation</note> pain.

**Likewise for the same.**

**A** fox’s liver does the same if it is drunk; it most effectively dispels the spleen [ailment].

**Against those who are short of breath.**

**G**ive a fox’s lung with dark wine to those who are short of breath <note>original: "suspiriosis." Refers to asthma or labored breathing</note>; it frees them most perfectly.

**Against parotids—that is, the small glands that are around the ear.**

**I**f you rub the <term>parotids</term> <note>swollen lymph nodes or mumps</note> often with a fox’s testicle, it immediately dissolves them.

**For healing the swelling of the groins.**

**I**n a similar way, do this same thing for the swellings <note>original: "paniculam." Likely referring to inflamed lymph nodes or buboes</note> as you did above; they will be healed.

**Against headache.**

**D**rink the same as you would for the headache above; it most perfectly dispels the headache.

**For swelling of the throat.**

**T**he <term>nature</term> <note>original: "natura." In this context, likely referring to the female genitalia or skin of the fox</note> of a fox wrapped around the head wonderfully takes away the pain of the throat.

**For the provocation of sexual intercourse.**

**T**he tip of a fox’s tail suspended from the arm [acts as] a provocation for sexual intercourse <note>an aphrodisiac charm</note>.

**Against ear pain.**

**F**ox gall dripped into the ears most perfectly takes away the pain of the ears.

**Against dimness of the eyes.**

**F**ox gall mixed with Attic honey <note>honey from the region of Athens, highly prized in antiquity for its purity</note> most perfectly dispels the dimness of the eyes.

**Likewise for ear pain.**

**M**elted fox fat dripped into the ears wonderfully takes away the earache.

**Against gout.**

**L**et the inner part of a shoe be lined with the skin of a fox, and let those suffering from <term>podagra</term> <note>gout, specifically in the feet</note> use it; they will have milder attacks.

# Regarding the ram, four virtues are held. First

<meta>This page begins a new section on the medicinal and symbolic properties of the ram (Aries), following a previous section on an unknown ailment.</meta>

->[Illustration of a ram with curled horns and a textured fleece]<-

<note>The ram, or *Aries*, was not only a common livestock animal but also the first sign of the zodiac, often associated with the head in medieval medicine.</note>

<summary>This page introduces a treatise on the four medicinal virtues of the ram, featuring a detailed illustration of the animal.</summary>

**H**orse saliva: <gloss>if</gloss> a person with a cough drinks it frequently, they will be made healthy.
<gloss>But where is this same thing found written for those who are unskilled? <note>original: "Hoc autem hoc idem scriptum reperiet do expertes sum?" - The glossator seems to be questioning the source or accessibility of the recipe.</note></gloss>
**H**orse foam: if <gloss>this [is applied] to an unpubescent boy</gloss> it binds the groin <note>likely referring to preventing pubic hair growth</note>: hair will not grow there. Likewise, they do not procreate.
<margin>For children's teeth.</margin>
**H**orse teeth: those which are first born <note>milk teeth</note>: if hung from the necks of infants, they are the greatest remedy for [teething] teeth.
<margin>For toothache.</margin>
**H**orse teeth: <gloss>from the tooth...</gloss> those which are first born, if you touch the teeth that ache: it will be a remedy.
<margin>For [the same?]</margin>
**H**orse [bit/bridle?]: wherever <gloss>it bites... conquered</gloss> they will not feel pain. If from there... for an infant's eye it may heal.
<margin>To heal pain of the spleen.</margin>
**H**orse tongue: dried and prepared. When you give it with wine to those suffering from the spleen to drink: it will be a great remedy.
<margin>For women who labor with [the womb].</margin>
**H**orse milk: when drunk, it does the same.
**H**orse milk: when drunk, it cleanses the womb most effectively. And in turn, it benefits one laboring with [the same].
<margin>For those with the falling sickness <note>Epilepsy</note>.</margin>
**H**orse milk: given to those with the falling sickness and drunk by them. We say it does the same as above.
<margin>For those who drink [poison from] a sea-hare.</margin>
**H**orse milk: [taken] against the sea-hare <note>A toxic marine slug, Aplysia, often cited in ancient toxicology</note>...
<margin>May they be saved.</margin>
**H**orse milk: <gloss>from this: which... fiber</gloss> when drunk... or from the sea-hare... the cure comes forth: the gall [taken] before drinking it induces sleep.
<margin>Against strangury <note>Difficulty in urinating</note>.</margin>
**H**orse gall: <gloss>...</gloss>

vinegar. And reduced to a powder and given as a drink: it profits wonderfully.
<margin>For the disease of childbirth.</margin>
**H**orse urine: if a woman is fumigated with it, the dead fetus will immediately exit and the afterbirth will follow.

<margin>Here begins the section on the Ram:</margin>

# Concerning the benefits of the ram. Thus they are held. The first cure of it is for curing certain fevers.

---

**F**rom the head of a ram: <gloss>and from the horns and the testicles.</gloss> Smoke some of the wool. <gloss>The yolk</gloss> tied and suspended... it immediately dispels tertian fevers <note>Malaria-like fevers recurring every three days</note>. Black wool of the same, dipped in water then oil, and suspended... takes away their pain... and if the womb is displaced, it may stay it... and the burnt wool in water... dispels [the ailment].
<margin>For the Royal Disease <note>Jaundice</note>.</margin>
**T**he grease <note>original: "sades" / "sordes"</note> of a ram: which is found between the thighs and the head: with birthwort <note>Aristolochia</note> and myrrh in equal weights, give it to the patient to drink. It works best for the Royal Disease.
<margin>For a callus.</margin>
**A** wolf <note>A type of ulcer or skin lesion</note> grows in the sinew or in the hands. In which... <gloss>remains</gloss> ram's dust <note>powdered horn or bone</note> shall drip: ... it heals all. Indeed, the powder cut small takes away bruises and discoloration: it makes scars hidden. And it frees feet injured at the heel from pain, and levels them surely.
<margin>Against the falling sickness <note>Epilepsy</note>.</margin>
**R**am's horn: a drachm <gloss>for the falling sickness</gloss> is given to those with the falling sickness; they ought to [take it] before they drink or eat. ... and to abstain from [wine?].

<margin>Here begins the section on the Goat.</margin>

# Concerning the goat there are 46 medicines. The first cure of it is for the "Holy Fires" <note>Erysipelas or shingles</note>.

---

**G**oat horns: crushed in flame with vinegar, if you rub it on. It completely heals the Holy Fire.
<margin>For sleep.</margin>
**G**oat horns: <gloss>to take the juice.</gloss> Place it under the head of a sick person who is not sleeping: they sleep immediately.
<margin>For the [falling sickness].</margin>
**G**oat dung: if... it should fall: ... and let it be smelled by the nostrils. When they have taken it: they will immediately fall if they have the falling sickness.
<margin>To put serpents to flight.</margin>
**G**oat horn: burnt and used as a fumigant, it will put to flight every kind of serpent.
<margin>For [sores/abscesses].</margin>
**G**oat horn: <gloss>...</gloss> they burn... and you smear it with flour. And apply it. It immediately subsides.
<margin>For falling hair.</margin>
**G**oat horn: burn it: and mix its soot with myrtle oil: then anoint the head: it retains falling hair and makes it grow sharply.
<margin>To heal strangury.</margin>
**G**oat hair: made into a fumigant, it acts against strangury and levels it.
<margin>For lethargy... for sleep.</margin>
**G**oat hair: ... and reduced to a very fine powder. And mixed with vinegar and placed in the nostrils: it immediately rouses those in lethargy: ...
<margin>For blood.</margin>
**G**oat fat: <gloss>lest it flow from the nostrils.</gloss> Mixed with myrtle and vinegar, apply it to the nostrils; it repressed the flowing blood in a moment.
<margin>For the [ears?].</margin>
**G**oat [fat/dung?]: <gloss>...</gloss> the head boiled in water with pitch—

<summary>This page details various folk and "sympathetic" medical remedies derived from horses, rams, and goats, covering ailments from epilepsy and jaundice to hair loss and difficult childbirth. It reflects the medieval tradition of using animal-based substances (zootherapy) as primary pharmacological ingredients.</summary>
<keywords>zootherapy, horse milk, ram's wool, goat horn, epilepsy, jaundice, tertian fever, medieval pharmacology, Sextus Placitus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a series of animal-based medical remedies, transitioning from goat-derived treatments to the medicinal uses of human urine and the hair of children.</meta>

...be smoked. We also say it is a remedy if given to be eaten. Indeed, the same flesh boiled in water prevents suppuration <note>The formation of pus</note>. It also cures stiffness of the neck, which some call *tetanus* <note>original: "ceruicũ distantit qd̃ quiadas quidam uocant"</note>.
<margin>For hair loss.</margin>
**G**oat hooves, burned and mixed with liquid pitch and applied, cure *alopecia* <note>patchy hair loss</note>. However, for all the things written above, the male goat has a more powerful efficacy, except that the female goat excels in milk alone.
<margin>Urine remedies begin, from a boy and a girl:</margin>

---

<margin>The first cure for white spots in the eyes.</margin>
**T**he urine of a boy and a girl is a healing wash; if you anoint the eyes, it thins out white spots <note>original: "albugine"</note> and cataracts <note>original: "eleucomatũ"</note> and makes their milky film not incurable. Indeed, to some extent, it soothes the cloudiness and the roughness of the corners of the eyes when washed six times.
<margin>For roughness of the eyes.</margin>
**I**f you anoint the eyes with the wash of a boy and a girl, after the anointing, it drives away scarring. Moreover, if poured into the ear—which was mentioned before—it will be a remedy for the whole body.
<margin>For stings from a bee, a wasp, or a hornet:</margin>
**I**f those who have been stung by a bee, a wasp, or a hornet are immediately bathed with the wash of a boy and a girl, it provides a remedy.
<margin>For gout.</margin>
**T**ake the first hair that a boy has had from birth and place it on the aching feet of those with gout; know that they will be healed.
<margin>So that a woman does not conceive.</margin>
**A** boy's tooth from his seventh year, better if set in gold or silver and suspended from the arm, prevents a woman from conceiving.
<margin>For healing those with leprosy.</margin>
**I**f those suffering from leprosy <note>original: "elefantiosos"</note> drink the wash of a boy and a girl, they will be healed. It even corrects leprosy when it is just beginning.
<margin>Also for soldering gold.</margin>
**I**f someone grinds the wash of a boy and a girl in a copper mortar with a copper pestle, he can solder gold with it.
<margin>For a woman so that she may conceive.</margin>
**I**f a woman carries the first urine a male infant emits when he is born, and applies it to her private parts, it brings about conception.
<margin>Against the bite of a dog.</margin>
**I**nfant's hair applied with vinegar heals a dog bite without swelling, preserves the wound, and corrects ulcers on the head.
<margin>For pain in the groin.</margin>
**I**f someone frequently touches their own genitals <note>original: "natura sua"</note>, it suppresses the pain; this same thing brings a remedy to the groin.
**I**f they bind the big toe of the foot together with the toe that is next to it...

### **His first cure: against mange of the head** <note>original: "allopici" - likely referring to alopecia or mange, conditions causing hair loss</note>

**T**ake the dry dung of a cat with equal parts mustard <note>original: "sinape" - mustard seed was a common warming agent in medieval medicine</note>

<summary>This manuscript fragment provides a medicinal recipe for treating hair loss or scalp conditions using a mixture of dried cat dung and mustard. It is a characteristic example of "dreckapotheke" or filth-pharmacopeia common in medieval medical traditions.</summary>

<page-num>58v - 59r</page-num>

<margin>Bird</margin>

### First cure: against hair loss of the head.
**F**resh cat dung soaked in vinegar with equal weights of mustard and rubbed into the places where the hair has fallen out heals me. <term>For discharge after childbirth</term> <note>original: "Ad profluvium ex partu"</note>.
**C**at dung with resin and rose placed as a suppository in the woman's place immediately restrains the discharge. <term>It is said for a bone in the throat</term> <note>original: "Inquit spina gulae"</note>.
**C**at dung should be ground and smeared on the throat of the patient: you can extract the bone without difficulty.
**C**at dung <term>Against quartan fevers</term> <note>A fever that recurs every four days, often associated with malaria</note>. Suspended from the neck and on the arm with an owl's claw, it drives away the quartan fevers after and during the onset: and from there it never hastens to release another.

### Experiments concerning <term>weasels</term> begin. <note>original: "hristlibuf", likely a corruption of "mustelis"</note>
<margin>It makes it soft with fire. If anyone from it...</margin>
**F**or those who do not sleep. They take the fat of a weasel and anoint the forehead. Immediately and without delay, they shall sleep.

### Experiments for each of the <term>weasels</term> begin.
### First cure against those suffering from <term>elephantiasis</term> <note>A term used in the Middle Ages for severe skin diseases, including leprosy</note>. From this for the most foul humor and its earth...
**K**ill weasels and burn the blood and flesh. Reduce it into a very fine ash. Then carefully strengthen that powder and blood together. Let him frequently smear the elephantiasis sufferers therewith: it restores or heals and perfectly cures.

### Experiments concerning <term>mice</term> begin.
**T**ake ten mice. <term>First against all pains of the eyes</term> <note>original: "ad omnes fi up adpeline dolores"</note>. Collect the heads with the ten tails and burn them together and reduce into a very fine powder. Mix all these things carefully with honey and for tears [apply] over the eyes with tallow or oil; they create a wonderful clarity of the eyes. <term>This medicine is forbidden</term> <note>original: "hoc medicamentum uetatit"</note>.

---

<page-num>59</page-num>

### First cure of the <term>bird</term>: for the Royal Disease <note>Jaundice</note>.
**T**he brain of a partridge given as a drink to the patient with a quantity of wine. It cures the Royal Disease wonderfully and smooths it.
<term>For beginning dimness of the eyes</term>: or <term>white spots and cataracts</term> <note>original: "albub; ⁊ leucomata"</note>.
**T**his composition is most certain. For we ourselves have proven it with great and frequent [use]. Take a spoonful of the bladder in which they are contained and <unclear>hopris</unclear> that is, if it has a dried ear. With opobalsamum <note>A rare aromatic resin</note> in two equal parts. Put two shells together while <unclear>gōdone</unclear>. And carefully mix all together in a <unclear>agrcum</unclear> vessel. This is most suitable to be wiped into the eyes. If it is replaced most cleanly. And from there frequently wipe the eyes. And if it often dries: <unclear>intra rem ceperieri l' ohtor que</unclear> so that he may see clearly. If he has an intact pupil: he will be freed without difficulty. This we ourselves have tried very frequently and it is a most true experiment.

### Experiments concerning the <term>raven</term> begin.
### Its first cure for blackening the hair:
**T**he brain of a raven will dye the hair in this way.
**A** raven's egg will dye the hair in this way.
<term>For the falling out of hair on the chin</term> <note>original: "Vnuis defluxionū in nafculo"</note>.
**T**he brain of a raven <unclear>in a copper vessel</unclear> and let it be moved for a long time until it changes color: then the head is shaved: then it is smeared. For a long time until the egg is consumed. However, he ought to hold oil in his mouth until it dries, lest the teeth also turn black. Afterwards the head should be bound. And after the fourth day let it be released. And this ensures they never grow [back].
<term>For discharge or infection</term>
**T**ake raven's brain. Bind it itself over the bread: this has often given [relief]: it will be sucked out immediately.
<term>Against the falling of infants</term> <note>Likely referring to epilepsy or "falling sickness"</note>.
**R**aven's dung with wool... if you combine it with remedies.
<term>For a toothache</term>
**R**aven's dung poured into the hollow of the tooth. If you place it... it immediately takes away the pain.

### The medicinal virtues of the <term>rooster</term> begin.
### First cure:
Rooster dung for the ears... or for those who have been weighed down by Jupiter or Mars <note>Astrological references to planetary influences on health</note>.
**T**he brain of a rooster drunk from a cup with added pepper is most beneficial: for those who were recently weighed down by Mars: provide the brain of a rooster with wine for drinking.

<meta>This page continues a series of medical recipes derived from animals, specifically focusing on the rooster (gallus) and the hen (gallina). It follows a common medieval tradition of "simples" where various parts of an animal are used to treat specific ailments.</meta>

...heals a rough bite most perfectly.

### To stop blood from fleeing the limbs
The **m**embrane of a rooster that contains the brain compresses and retains flowing blood. Indeed, if anyone uses it to wipe away <term>telangenis</term> <note>likely a corruption of 'telangiectasias' or similar skin eruptions</note>, that is, the "sedges" <note>original: carices</note> where they occur, it dismisses them. It also works for <term>suffusion</term> <note>cataracts</note> and <term>leukoma</term> <note>white spots on the cornea</note>.

### For [pain] of the testicles
Give the **t**esticles of a rooster with water and milk to patients to drink while fasting. However, let them abstain from wine for [some] days. It is a greatest remedy for the "falling sickness" <note>epilepsy</note>. Indeed, the testicles ought to be kept dry, and when there is a need, they are taken in a well-prepared drink.

### To excite sexual intercourse
Place the **t**esticles of a rooster with goose fat in a ram's skin, and keep it suspended from your arm. It excites intercourse wonderfully. Furthermore, if they smear the bed with the blood itself, it ensures that those who are quiet do not [fail] in intercourse.

### For suffocation of the womb <note>A historical term for hysteria or uterine displacement</note>
The **i**nner membrane of a rooster's gizzard, when taken in a pottage with wine, most powerfully corrects and smooths the succession of the "little ears" <note>original: auricularii; likely referring to uterine appendages or related pains</note>.

### For a swollen belly
Take the **h**en's aforementioned membrane with <term>atringia</term> <note>an astringent substance</note>, and likewise give it as a drink in warm oil. It closes up flabby bellies. With its help, it casts out the "evil humor" which they call <term>authemes</term>.

### For those with stones
Give the **c**loaca <note>the posterior orifice</note> of a rooster as a drink in the same manner as above. It will be the greatest remedy for those with stones; it breaks the stones without delay and ejects the urine.

### For hemorrhoids <gloss>For hemorrhoids</gloss>
You quickly release the **v**ent of a rooster for [the sake of] Venus <note>sexual health</note>; or if it has been drunk, you quickly release Venus. You should place into its belly <term>phlegms</term> <note>original: dregmas</note> etc. in the place you seek. Afterwards, let him put salt or a sieve into its belly.

### For intercourse
**L**ikewise for the face, if you do not dread the salt. As I wrote above, it will be an excellent remedy for those who are worn out regarding **i**ntercourse.

### Against rabid dogs
The **t**hyme <note>likely the thymus gland</note> of a rooster <gloss>and it boils</gloss> heals and cures when placed upon the flesh beforehand, and it dispels the fragments.

### For the [loss] of hair <gloss>they cut the fox</gloss> <note>original: uulpe secant; a reference to alopecia, literally 'fox-mange'</note>
If a **h**en [is applied] to the face for those who suffer, that is, those who have head pains. After the feathers have been plucked and cut, and bound in a linen cloth, and he himself has gathered it to his ear, it will fly away: he will be healed.

### To consolidate [broken] vessels
The **s**even [parts] of a hen, and all vessels otherwise broken, if they are treated, it consolidates them in a marvelous way.

### Here begin the virtues of the hen
<insert>The first cure</insert>
### Of the hen itself for stings of the eyes
A **h**en's egg is the newest [remedy] if one has an egg-stall. If the white from it is poured into aching eyes, it settles all pain and stings.

### To restrain tears of the eyes

---

**F**rom one liquid [part], a little myrtle with manna of frankincense; and you place it in hen's wool <note>likely down feathers</note>: you apply it. This contains flowing tears and brings sleep if an ointment of oil is added.

### For a cough
A **h**en's egg, [given] weak and raw to those coughing, will be a good remedy.

### Again for the same
Boil the **e**ggs of a hen and [use for] fistulas, with sulfur and sour urine added. It is especially profitable for coughing infants.

### For the "Sacred Fire" <note>Erysipelas or shingles</note>
Smear the **b**ody where it burns with a raw hen's egg, and place leaves of betony on top.

### Against itchiness
Mix the **b**ones of a hen with lime and oil, and let the itchiness be gently dusted.

### For a woman in difficult labor <gloss>woman</gloss>
Give a **h**en's egg with a little rue, dry dill, and cumin mixed with wine to drink for two hours; while they drink, if it has been extracted, she will give birth without difficulty.

### Against [intestinal] worms
Break a **h**en's egg and put it from vessel to vessel, adding half a drachm of fat and one drachm of <term>resyptiaci</term> <note>an Egyptian salve</note> and five obols of honey. All these shall be mixed into one. Let him carry the vessel warm in the morning so it may be suspended. When he has nodded to those wishing to [drink], drink with wine or water.

### For a woman's discharge <gloss>woman</gloss>
Take an **e**gg and burn it, and mix it in wine, and smear the walls of the place <note>the vagina</note> from it: it is very beneficial.

### For canker <gloss>and sores on the feet</gloss>
Mix two **w**hites of hen's eggs <gloss>skillfully</gloss> and two drachms of <term>aretani</term> and a little myrtle together; likewise smear the body where it is blind <note>closed or hidden</note>: you will experience the highest result.

### Concerning spots that are born from the sun
**H**en's eggs are born from the sun. Macerated in vinegar so that the whole shell appears as a membrane. When opened so the white flows out: as much as is needed, flour is added, and if there is any, frankincense. Then all these ground together are contained in one [part] honey. Then the face is smeared.

### For a fresh wound of the body
The **w**hite of hen's eggs, if mixed with frankincense and [other ingredients], is beneficial to be dried in the sun.

### To consolidate vessels
The **w**hite of a hen's egg mixed with quicklime consolidates glass vessels.

### For every pain of the belly
**H**en's eggs with milk and saffron: afterwards applied to the woman's place and polished: they are wonderfully beneficial.

### Again <gloss>warm</gloss>
A **h**en's egg with raisin wine, that is, in wine: collected in wool, they do the same as above.

### For every eye pain
A **h**en's egg mixed as above and applied to the eye takes away all pain of the eyes.

### To heal swollen places
**H**en's eggs with boar's fat, seed, and pearl barley [applied] to the places of those suffering.

<margin>
Against
a long-lasting
cough
</margin>
**E**ggs of birds <note>original: "Oua ouorum"</note>, if the shells are broken and hung nearby, are most powerful at chasing away a <unclear>cough</unclear>. For burning of the stomach with cold: **E**ggs of birds are <unclear>troublesome</unclear> to the stomach, but may be drunk from the shell <unclear>from</unclear> the top if tempered. For those who [spit] blood: take the raw yolk of a smoky egg, for when it is mixed with three parts of wine, it must be beaten. For bruises and swellings: **E**ggs of birds with Greek marrow <note>likely a specific medicinal preparation</note> cooked and applied as a poultice takes away all pains. For those who cannot keep food down: **E**ggs, boil the yolk for two hours until dissolved, and having fasted, take one part with oil; afterwards take it and sprinkle <unclear>pepper</unclear> on it; the smell is most powerfully beneficial. For the flux of a woman's womb: **E**ggs of birds mixed with oil, applied to the woman's area in the manner of a <term>pessary</term> <note>a medicinal plug</note>, restrains the flow. For pustules of the eye: **E**ggs of birds, [using] the fat, applied to eyes having pustules, they press the pustules out. For healing eye pain: **E**ggs of birds, [and] the feet of a hen cut off and boiled with salt <unclear>and water</unclear> and given: the eye pain will soon be taken away. For a <term>carbuncle</term> <note>original: "prunone," a burning skin infection</note> and for hen dung: against a carbuncle and likewise for the sting of scorpions, it works wonders for all cures.

---

[Treatments]. The first cure for all serpent bites:
**A** live dove, or a blackbird, or a <term>moeta</term> <note>possibly a gull or similar water bird</note> applied [to the wound]: it works best and heals every bite of serpents. For the pooling of blood flowing from the nose: **D**ove's blood, fresh and applied repeatedly, is most powerfully beneficial. However, the blood ought to be boiled in a small metal dish with honey: mixed together and placed over the eyes, it is as beneficial for health as <unclear>popenis</unclear>. For the effusion of blood flowing from the nose: **D**ove's blood, which matches the members of the brain itself, checks the flowing blood. **D**ove's [dung] for all [ailments]: dung from live ones to be brought out... <note>The text here becomes fragmented with abbreviations</note> and discussed with the mouth. Therefore, before them, use oil to foment the place. Doing this: as if around the eyes... and does not allow <term>scars</term> <note>original: "destugias"</note> to be born from these same falls as they are wont to do. Concerning the goose... they begin. The first cure of it: for the ear in which [water has entered].

<margin>
Goose
</margin>
**G**rease or fat mixed with onion, poured into the ear in which water has entered, provides relief. **G**oose fat is a remedy for <term>phalanges</term> <note>spider bites</note> and with honey and <term>tartar</term> <note>potassium bitartrate</note>: and [in a] shell: and the smear is the best remedy for <term>angina</term> <note>throat inflammation</note>.

The virtues
of the swallow

The first cure for <unclear>cussiellas</unclear> and beans. The genus of the swallow: the chicks... and reduced into the finest ash are most beneficial for diseases of the throat and swellings. For wounds of the tongue and lips: **R**eeds <note>or perhaps swallow-parts, "arundines" vs "hirundines"</note> in much honey... for those sustaining [wounds] of the tongue or lips, it is applied with deadly strength <note>likely meaning "extremely effective"</note>.

### Here begins the book of Dioscorides Innocentus
In this book are contained sixty-one female herbs, most useful for the practice of medicine, with the rules for their prescriptions. The name of the first herb is this:

**C**oncerning the Golden Herb <note>likely *Teucrium polium* or similar</note>:
It works for those with spitting of blood, for those with sciatica, for those with stomach trouble, and for provoking urine. For bruises and for toothaches. For spasms of the chin. For the bite of a serpent. To put serpents to flight. The name of the second herb is <term>Bugloss</term>.

**C**oncerning the herb <term>Glotis</term>:
It produces cheerfulness... The name of the third herb is <term>Acutiyay</term> <note>possibly a corruption of a Greek plant name</note>.

**C**oncerning the [True] herb: it works for burns and lacerations. It moves the bowels and urine. It cures the bladder. It heals veins that have grown thick and those that have burst. The name of the fourth herb is <term>Heliochrysos</term> <note>Everlasting flower</note>.

**C**oncerning the herb: it works against worms and moves the bowels. It heals the bite of a shrew-mouse. It stops the blood of wounds. Mixed with old [wine?], it purges rot. It heals <unclear>glitincie</unclear>. It treats the itching of poison and the fetus. The name of the fifth herb is <term>Cinnomori</term> <note>possibly Peony or a type of Cinquefoil</note>.

**C**oncerning the herb: it works for inflammations of the <term>stomach</term>, it benefits those who are short of breath. It heals the strikes of serpents. It cures the <unclear>testui</unclear>. [It regulates] the humors of a woman, likewise it stops blood... it draws out the <term>afterbirth</term> <note>original: "pasticem"</note> from the body.
The name of this herb is <term>Chamaeleon</term>.

### The name of this herb is 30. Ox-eye.
<note>original: "Buftalmos" (Buphthalmos)</note>

**T**his herb is effective for clearing up films over the eyes <note>likely cataracts or leucoma</note>; it dissolves hardness, heals discharges, and restores the natural color to the eyes.

### The name of this herb is 208. Sideritis.
<note>Ironwort; often used for treating wounds caused by iron weapons</note>

**T**his herb cures fevers that come with shivering. It treats those suffering from hydrophobia <note>fear of water caused by rabies</note> and restrains the madness of dogs; it is very effective.

### The name of this herb is 19. Hypreos.
<note>likely a variant of Hypericum or St. John's Wort</note>

**T**his herb stops blood flowing from the nostrils. It benefits those with dysentery and promotes the flow of urine. It heals fresh wounds. It is useful for those with bladder stones, those suffering from fluxes, and for ruptured veins within the cornea and between the eyes, or wherever ruptures of the bladder occur.

### The name of this herb is 33. Ayzos.
<note>Aizoon or Houseleek</note>

**T**his herb settles "sacred fire" <note>erysipelas, a skin infection</note>, herpes, inflammations of the eyes, burns, and gouty pain. It mitigates headaches and cures the bites of venomous spiders <term>spalangionu: venomous spiders or insects</term>. It heals dysentery, fluxes of the belly, and intestinal worms. It inhibits the "rosy humor" <note>reddish discharge</note> and flows from the womb in women. It is powerful for aching eyes.

### The name of this herb is 32. Cynorropinas.
<note>likely Dog-rose or a similar briar</note>

**T**his herb is used for pain of the spleen, for headaches, and for cankers <note>ulcerous sores</note>.

### The name of this herb is 36. Scolymbos.
<note>Golden Thistle or Artichoke</note>

**T**his herb draws out foul urine from the belly and the body; it is also a healthy food.

---

### The name of this herb is 37. Achillea.
<note>Yarrow; named after Achilles, who supposedly used it to heal his soldiers</note>

**T**his herb takes away the pain of fresh wounds and represses bleeding. It is excellent for bonding wounds or for a womb flowing with blood. It extinguishes the humors of women flowing from the natural places. It is beneficial for those with dysentery.

### The name of this herb is 38. Stafisagria.
<note>Stavesacre or Lice-bane</note>

**T**his herb purges the body of humors that have arrived there; it heals "sirialism" <note>likely heatstroke or inflammation</note>, itchiness, and the scabies. It purges the brain of humors. It cures toothache, defects of the gums, and all types of rot.

### The name of this herb is 39. Cameles.
<note>likely Chamaeleon thistle</note>

**T**his herb loosens the belly. It draws out phlegm and bile. It cures filthy and septic wounds.

### The name of this herb is 40. Ericios.
<note>possibly Heath or Heather</note>

**T**his herb cures the bites of serpents. It settles pain in the loins and provides milk to the breasts.

### The name of this herb is 41. Spleenwort.
<note>original: "Splenion"</note>

It removes [ailments of] the spleen and resolves difficulty of urine. It cures the "black disease" <note>melancholy or black jaundice</note> and is beneficial for hiccups and those with bladder stones. It absorbs what it touches.

### The name of this herb is 42. Cycinallos.

**T**his herb works to bind the belly, for vomiting, for thinning the hair, and for fistulas. It is used for loose teeth, for "arnes" <note>unclear; possibly skin growths</note>, for carbuncles, for "sagnes" <note>unclear</note>, and for "syringues" <note>fistulous sores</note>.

### The name of this herb is 43. Licorice.
<note>original: "Gliziriza"</note>

**T**his herb works for roughness of the throat, for taking away burning fevers, for the chest, for the belly, for the care of the internal organs, for quenching thirst, and for...

---

And its flower sends forth a purple color, in which there is a seed similar to a thistle, both round and small. The root of this, dried and powdered: if two spoonfuls are given to drink with water, it benefits those with spitting of blood, those with liver trouble, and those with stomach ailments. It also provokes urine. If the succulent parts are pounded and applied as a poultice, it removes humors. A decoction of it settles the pain of teeth if held in the mouth. For infants who suffer in the palate: let its seed be ground and given to drink in water; it helps. The same drink is effective against the bites of serpents. Some even wish to pray with it hanging [around the neck]: it puts every serpent to flight. Indeed, the name of this following herb is: Bugloss. <note>Ox-tongue</note>

### Bugloss is so called because it has rough leaves in the manner of an ox's tongue.

**T**his herb is also called "Sibyl" by some; it has rough, dark leaves spread on the ground. If put into wine, it makes the mind cheerful. It is taken as a vegetable in food and decoctions, or used as a seasoning.

### The name of this herb is called Adurion.
<note>original: "adurion" - likely Centaury or a similar species</note>

Some indeed call it "Black-hair" <note>original: "melanchelylon"</note>. Others call it "Five-leaf" <note>original: "Penteron"</note>. It grows in pleasant and watery places, as well as in stony ones. It has broad leaves, a little larger than lettuce leaves, divided like rocket, of a blackish green. It has a stem seven years <note>likely a scribal error for "inches" or "parts"</note> or a cubit long, as thick as a finger. In the upper part at the very head, it has smaller, oblong leaves, between which seeds grow in the manner of parrots <note>likely referring to the shape of the seed pod</note>, and from which whitish flowers emerge. The head of the herb itself is similar to a thyrsus <note>a ritual staff</note>. The roots are soft, flaccid, and sticky, with a slow taste. For sores: this herb, when crushed and applied as a poultice, heals burns and lacerations. Its root, dried, sifted, and drunk in warm water, moves the belly and the urine. It also helps those with consumption <note>original: "Ptyficos"</note> and those who suffer spasms, or those who...

# The name of the herb Canae: it is called Camerops.
<note>original: "Camerops" - likely Wall Germander (Teucrium chamaedrys).</note>

<margin>
It heals everything. A potion of this, diligently ground with oil and applied to the eyes, very quickly clears away their dimness.
</margin>

This herb grows in rough or stony places. From a single root, and with a single appearance, it gathers many stalks; it is short and small with very tiny leaves, and small reddish spots. It is warming by nature. It should be collected at the time when it is in seed. The juice expressed from it while raw, or in a decoction, is beneficial for fistulas <note>tubular sores or ulcers</note>. Or if it is diligently prepared as a pill, it is effective for those who have suffered injuries to the membranes <note>original: "omenta" - the lining of the internal organs</note> from some blow. It succors those with asthma, or those who pass urine with pain and difficulty. It is as beneficial as hyssop for those with lung ailments. It provokes the menses. It soon expels a dead fetus from the womb. It cleanses the spleen if taken with vinegar, which thins it. Applied to the bites of serpents, or drunk with wine, it is beneficial. With honey and myrrh, it purges other ulcers.

---

# The name of this herb is Polygonos.
<note>original: "Polygonos" - Knotgrass (Polygonum aviculare).</note>

<insert>
It is most beneficial for ulcers and for those whose genitals drip with moisture; the pure juice applied with wool restrains the flow. For aching ears, or those flowing with discharge, it offers very swift help. A decoction of this herb mixed with honey heals ulcers of the private parts if it is poured in through a tube. Its leaves, plucked beforehand and placed on the stomach, soothe its heat and the burning of the blood for those who are suffering. It is healthfully applied to recent wounds, for they mitigate "Holy Fire" <note>original: "Ignem sacrum" - likely Erysipelas or St. Anthony's Fire, a skin infection</note>.
</insert>

By some it is called *polycarpos*, or *catemeron*, or *deonice*, or *mirtopetala*. This herb is so thin here that it spreads out like grass. It has leaves similar to rue, but longer and softer. In each leaf it contains seed and produces a white or rosy flower. It has constricting and drying properties. The expressed juice of this herb, given with pure wine in equal weights of water, heals those spitting blood <note>original: "emptoricis"</note>, looseness of the belly, and those with cholera; it is most powerfully beneficial to those who pass urine with difficulty. It also provides effective aid against the bites of serpents. For tertian fevers, it should be drunk an hour before the onset of the shivering fit.

---

# The name of this herb is called Sansuion.
<note>original: "Sansuion" - Marjoram (Origanum majorana).</note>

Let it be applied to gatherings <note>abscesses</note> so that they may break. Used as a vaginal suppository for women, they move the menses. Marjoram ground with salt and vinegar and applied as a poultice heals. For the same reason, it and myrrh are profitable for the twisting of the joints; indeed, it mitigates the heat and tumors of the eyes in a wonderful way when applied as a poultice with soda <note>original: "niytra"</note> meal.
</margin>

This same herb of Syria grows by creeping along the ground. It has soft, woolly, and round leaves, similar to catnip. It has a good scent and a warming nature. A drink of the decoction itself restrains the beginnings of dropsy <note>edema</note>; it is beneficial for difficulties of urine and the twisting of the intestines. Truly, the dry leaves of this herb, ground and mixed with honey...

This same herb produces stalks a cubit high and the thickness of a finger; it has fleshy leaves the length of fingers, in the shape of a tongue. If rubbed on, it begins to soothe the coldness more powerfully. When drunk with wine or warm water, it acts against the bite of spiders <note>original: "phalangiorum", referring to venomous spiders</note>. Likewise for menstrual flow and intestinal worms. It alone causes the purging of women's reproductive organs. It constricts the moisture of the air.
**The name of this herb: Aristolochia.** <note>Also known as Birthwort, historically used to aid childbirth.</note>

---

<margin>or fuffoessa</margin>
**B** They say this herb is best for the needs of women. For if a woman sits over it in a prostrate position, by the benefit of the mother <note>likely referring to the "mother" or womb</note> it purges the vapors. Indeed, there are two kinds of this herb. One which is called "long" and is masculine. The other is "round," with leaves similar to ivy. It has a good scent, somewhat liquid with a certain bitterness; it has a white flower and a round root like a turnip. The long one, which is the male, has a purple flower, longer leaves, and a long root. Its leaves, crushed and applied to the genitals, are most powerfully effective against the bites of serpents. Likewise, its root provides the same if it was the cause. And reduced into a very soft powder, one drachm <term>drachma: a small unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams</term> drunk, it brings forth the fetus, just as when drunk with pepper, myrrh, and warm water. It purges the womb and brings out the adhering fetus. The round one does this same thing in all cases. It tames putrefaction and those who are short of breath, specifically the splenetic, and those who have ruptured anything. It heals the places of those in pain when given with warm water. The same applied to wounds removes splinters and bandages <note>original: "ligutas", likely referring to foreign objects or bindings</note> and broken bones. It cuts away rot and purges filthy wounds <gloss>that is, a cloth or bandage</gloss> and with <insert>added</insert> honey <gloss>mixed</gloss> with incense it cures the same; and in turn, it cleans and smooths the teeth and gums. <margin>The name of the thirteenth herb: it is called Sircas.</margin> <note>Likely Circaea or Enchanter's Nightshade.</note>

**B** This herb has some seed, not much, except that it is larger. Drunk, it is accustomed to clear the way for many things. **The name of this fourteenth herb is Diana.** <margin>it is called</margin>

---

**A** By some it is called Politerion, by others Salitricos. It has leaves similar to coriander, a long black stem of two spans <note>about 18 inches</note>. It has neither flower nor seed; its root prevails. A drink of this herb helps those who are short of breath, the hysterical <note>referring to "suffocation of the womb"</note>, and those who have difficulty urinating. It moves the menses and makes one unaware of stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>. It <gloss>collects</gloss> and stops the flux of the bowels. Crushed and applied with wine as a plaster, it is good for the stomach. It heals burning diseases and serpents. It dissolves hair loss <note>alopecia</note> and skin eruptions <gloss>sores/scabs</gloss>. It wipes away rashes and scabs from the head: crushed with myrrh, labdanum, myrtle oil, and litharge in wine, it holds falling hair. It does the same if it is cooked with wine and oil, and the head is washed with that same liquid.
**The name of this herb: Female Mandrake.**

---

<margin>the female mandrake</margin>

**B** This same herb many call "Apollinaris" or "Apple of the Earth." There are two kinds of this herb: male and female. The male has white, large leaves. Likewise, it has fruits <note>original: "mala", apples</note> the size of large apples. The power of both is the same. The fresh leaves of this herb with barley meal are good for the heat of the eyes. They dissolve and purge the veins in wounds in that same heat, and every collection of hardness. The same, lightly rubbed on the marks of the body for seven days, removes them without ulceration. The same, polished with salt, persists for a long time and provides a pleasant appetite in all things. Its root crushed with vinegar cures "sacred fire" <note>erysipelas or shingles</note>. With honey or with oil, it is useful against the bite of serpents.

The female mandrake
</margin>

**B**y some it is called *polytrichos* <note>original: "politricos"; likely Maidenhair fern, literally "many-haired"</note>, and by others *callitrichos* <note>original: "callitricos"; literally "beautiful-haired"</note>. It has leaves similar to Coriander <note>original: "Coliandro"</note>, and a long, black stem measuring two spans <note>A "span" is the distance from the tip of the thumb to the tip of the little finger, roughly 9 inches</note>, but it bears no flower.

**T**his same herb is called by many "Apollinaris" <note>The herb of Apollo</note>, or the **Ma**... <note>The text breaks off here as it begins to describe the Mandrake, or *Mandragora*</note>

<column-break/>

<meta>This page continues the medicinal applications of plants from the previous section, specifically focusing on Mandrake (implied by the opening text regarding anesthesia), Thapsia, Water Mint (Sytimber), and Celandine. When they dissolve scrofulous swellings <note>original: "chytadas," likely referring to scrofula or glandular tumors</note> with water: with its power it soothes the pain of the eyes. Furthermore, take three pounds of the bark of its root. Let them put it into an amphora with a fermented sweet wine. They set it aside so that it may mature for medicinal use. And if one drinks three small cups <note>term: "cyathos" (a standard Roman liquid measure, about 0.045 liters)</note> and one ounce of the wine, he drinks the seed... <note>The text here is somewhat fragmented or corrupted in the original Latin</note> because of this treatment, I do not feel the cutting of the upper part safely. However, if its apples <note>The fruit of the mandrake</note> are either smelled or eaten, they cause sleep and affect the body so much that they take away the voice. Also, the juice from its root is squeezed out; placed in an earthen vessel in the sun or cooked over a gentle fire, it should be stirred constantly until it has cooked to the thickness of honey. Afterward, it should be stored in a cool place for medicinal use. The roots are also kept dry, as they will be useful for many purposes: for the heat of scrofulous swellings, for the heat of <unclear>muclruu</unclear>, for dispersing hard collections of fluid, for the "holy fire" <note>term: "ignem sacrum" (Erysipelas, a skin infection)</note>, for the bite of a serpent, for scrofula, and for the pain of jaundice. Moreover, a potion of this herb is taken so that he who is being cut with iron <note>i.e., undergoing surgery</note> does not feel the pain.

<margin>Among the Greeks</margin>
# The name of this herb (21): Thapsis or Thapsos.
This herb has narrow leaves, as long as a finger and alone, flowing toward the ground. It has a long stalk, and the seed grows in a spike along the whole stalk. It is a bitter-tasting herb of a loving nature <note>original: "nature amantice," possibly a corruption of "aphrodisiac" or "warming"</note>. For one who has a <unclear>cepressus</unclear> <note>likely a tumor or growth</note> the size of a chickpea, drinking it for two days, or if it is rubbed on, it buries the tumors. An injection of it by means of a clyster <note>term: "clisterum" (an enema)</note> is beneficial for discharges. It aids the menses and the "whiteness" of women <note>likely leucorrhea</note>, the collection of fluids, and the rupture of internal organs. It certainly causes pregnant women to miscarry.

---

### The name of this herb (17): Sytimber <note>Water Mint or Sisymbrium</note>

This herb is stinging <note>original: "vrica," likely "urtica"</note> and germinating. Its seed, when drunk with wine, is immediately beneficial to those suffering from difficulty of urination. It also settles the gas and the twisting <note>colic</note> in the intestines. Its leaves, crushed and applied to the stomach, mitigate the pain of the twisting. It is also suitable to be used against the venom of wasps. Thus is its name found.

---

### The name of this herb (27): Called Celidonia <note>Celandine</note>

---

This herb is called Celidonia <note>from the Greek 'chelidon' for swallow</note> because it is seen to break forth at the arrival of the swallows. For it is said that if the eyes of the swallows' chicks are taken away, their mothers heal them with this herb. It has a saffron-colored juice and a biting, heavy odor. From this same juice, cooked gently with honey in a bronze pot over coals, it breaks the darkness of the eyes and clears them. It is pounded at the beginning of summer, and the juice is squeezed out and dried in the shade. From this, little tablets <note>term: "pastilli" (medicinal lozenges)</note> are made which will save the eyes. Moreover, the juice of the root tempered in white water <note>or white wine</note> cures and heals those with jaundice or "rusty" skin.

---

Indeed, this herb has the smell of mallow. The same is called by some "In the Lands" or "Etanthimos" <note>likely Anthemis/Chamomile</note>, so called because of its perennial flowers. There are three kinds of this herb, differing only in the flower, and all have one power. Each is a span in length, with many branches and small, round, goat-like leaves. For it is seen with flowers like ants <note>original: "mirmisecis," likely referring to the shape or appearance of the flower centers</note> and white on the outside. It is found in open places and near roads. This same herb should be collected in the springtime. It has a germinating and seeding quality. Drinking the water it was boiled in, or sitting over it <note>as a steam bath</note>, moves the urine, causes bladder stones to be dispersed, and provokes the menses. It heals swellings in the intestines and sores. Drunk, it also heals jaundice. It finds a cure for the kidneys and is beneficial for those with chronic heat. For it has its own efficacy, which is in the purple flower, and of all...

**Cetopas**, that is: those who have redness in the corners of the eyes in the manner of a chronic discharge, she cures with her own poultice. ### **T**he name of this herb is Mullein <note>original: "flomus"; Verbascum thapsus</note>.

**T**his herb is long and white. There are two kinds of medicinal value, male and female. The male indeed has longer and narrower leaves than the female. Both are woolly, they have a woolly stalk and long roots, the thickness of a finger, with a sharp taste. In this juice, for those who have lost their virility, this herb ground with wine is beneficial; or the same decocted and drunk: it either heals a suspended poison or is effective in a drink against a vision <note>possibly hallucinations or "evil eye"</note>. The same indeed, if it is held in the mouth, heals the pain of the teeth; its leaves placed outside upon the legs and eyes soothe curvatures and pains. The same, decocted with wine and honey, cures old swellings and wool-workers' sores; decocted in vinegar, they are useful for dying wounds. It is effective against the bite of a scorpion; this same herb, most diligently ground and cast upon the top of the ears. For they say it is called **Agria**, having long twigs and very small leaves, and a lovely flower...

---

...which they say makes hair gray. Another kind which are called "flowers of the earth," with a fig and the coming sun. A third has three or four red and woolly leaves; it has the power to make...

### **T**he name of this herb. It is called Linozostis <note>Mercury herb</note>.

**B**ut this herb is called by some the "Herb of Mercury." This herb has leaves similar to Groundsel <note>original: "Seneco"</note>, with two narrow branches; from between the branches it sends out other small shoots bearing seeds; the female indeed is in the manner of a cluster of grapes. They grow on those very branches. Both indeed are dried, and taken as a vegetable, they relax the belly. A decoction of this herb, when drunk, will lead out the bile with the humor. A poultice indeed applied removes all and scatures the rages; the name of this herb will be **Amberenon**, as they report.

---

**T**his herb is called by some **Cynocefalum** <note>Dog's Head; likely Snap-dragon or a similar species</note>. It is branched, and through the branches it has small and oblong and toothed leaves. The flower is small or waxy. The seed is green, similar to nostrils. The juice of this herb is pressed out, and the body is anointed with it, or the herb itself suspended on the body prohibits poisons, sorceries, or any similar things of this kind and all chest ailments. It renders a person beautiful.

### **T**he name of this herb is called Cynocefalum.

**B**rammis, this herb some call **Damosomos**; it has leaves truly like...

# Folio 48v

...similar, but blacker and woolly. In taste, it is astringent. It has a stem that is not large, and a root that is thin and short. The leaves of this herb are crushed and squeezed; its juice is thickened by the sun or by fire. It is stored for medicinal use so that when it is needed, it may be dissolved in wine or water to induce vomiting. It most effectively cures all rot of the mouth and throat, especially because its power is astringent. The name of this herb, number 25, is called <term>Psillios</term> <note>original: "Psillios"; likely Fleawort (Plantago psyllium), named for its seeds which resemble fleas.</note>.

---

<insert>
Some call this herb <term>cynomia</term> <note>Greek: "dog-fly"</note> because it has seeds like a flea. They also call the same "cynomia" the flea-herb. It has small, hairy leaves and a branched stem. When the whole plant is dry and brittle, it sends out a golden tuft from the middle. At the top, there are two or three heads in which the seed is black, similar to fleas. It grows in cultivated places. It has a cold quality.
</insert>

---

A full acetabulum <note>a Roman liquid measure, about 0.06 liters</note> of this seed is turned into two sextarii <note>about 1 liter</note> of water; let it soak and boil. Applied to the body as a plaster, it brings about a cure. It heals all gatherings <note>abscesses or tumors</note>. For headaches, it is used with rose oil. It also cures and flattens those protrusions where the navel sticks out or those swellings that occur under the armpits. The name of this herb, number 26, is called <term>Agelend aquitundam</term> <note>likely a corruption of a Germanic or local name for a species of Clematis or similar vine</note>.

It has leaves truly similar to a vine, but it is smaller in all respects than the white vine, which it resembles. If they reach maturity, they turn black. It has a root that is black on the outside and yellow-colored on the inside. Its shoots are boiled as a vegetable and they strongly move the urine. It reduces swelling of the spleen. It benefits those with eye inflammations and...
</insert>

# Folio 49r

...and is beneficial for those with stomach problems. It is useful in almost all the same ways as the white vine, though perhaps less effectively. It also helps when applied as a poultice. The name of this herb, number 27, is called <term>Tribulosa</term> <note>Puncturevine (Tribulus terrestris), named for its "tribulus" or caltrop-shaped thorny fruit.</note>.

---

<insert>
This herb is called "tribulosa" because of its quality. There are two kinds: one that grows on the mountain, and another that is wild, which is more effective. It has leaves similar to purslane, and it has branches and stems, but they are spread out upon the ground. On these, it has certain hard, thorny, and angular globules in which the seed is enclosed. This herb, "tribulosa," has an astringent and cold quality. Indeed, this herb, when crushed and applied to the body, soothes inflammations. Used as a drink mixed with honey, it cures all rot of the mouth and throat. Its green seed, crushed and drunk, benefits those with bladder stones.
</insert>

---

If a viper has bitten someone, let him drink one drachma—that is, three scruples—of the dried seed of this plant. Furthermore, if the herb itself is crushed with the seed and placed on the wound, it delivers the person from danger. Its seed drunk with wine is a health-giving potion against poison. This same herb "tribulosa," when carried with its seed, kills fleas; likewise if the house is sprinkled with water from it. This herb, while green, is fodder for Thracian horses, and they even make bread from its seed. The name of this herb, number 28, is <term>Loton</term> <note>Lotus; here likely referring to a species of Trefoil or Melilot.</note>.

# Page 71
## Of Poppies <note>original: "papiveris"</note>

...to heal the bites of serpents. For wounds and all [ailments], it moderates. The flowers and their leaves, crushed and drunk in wine, move the menstrual flow of women. It heals the difficulty of labor and difficulty of urine. For dizziness <note>original: "currigine"</note> and to heal the bite of a tiger. With vinegar [applied] before: it assists epileptics, that is, those with the falling sickness. The same herb boiled in water and placed beneath to steam the womb purifies it most perfectly. Its juice, introduced into the genitals with wool, causes abortion for those struggling [with pregnancy]. Truly, the oil in which it was cooked, if the body is anointed with it, repels cold fevers. Its leaves, applied as a plaster, soothe pain of the head.

### The name of this herb is called Stirgno. <note>Likely Nightshade (Solanum)</note>
<gloss>Others call it Ayantichos</gloss> <gloss>Others Acabulum</gloss> <gloss>Others Atitunu</gloss> <gloss>Others call it mammacos.</gloss>

---

**This** herb, Stirgnos, has leaves similar to basil <note>original: "oclimi"</note> except they are larger. The stalk, however, when it has grown, leans toward the ground. It has seed among the leaves along the branches, arranged at small intervals in little pods which are like bladders, in which they are of a reddish color; it is very smooth and overcomes great cold. The leaves of this, applied as a plaster, soothe the "sacred fire" <note>Erysipelas, a skin infection</note>. If any herpes <note>skin eruptions</note> show themselves through the body as pustules, this herb mixed with pearl barley and applied as a plaster <unclear>cleanses</unclear> and most perfectly wipes away the suffocating [sores]. Also, pain of the head, burning of the stomach, and parotid swellings <note>swelling of the salivary glands</note>, it dissolves when crushed with oil and applied. Its juice mixed with rose oil and applied heals the pain of ears. Likewise, when placed under the genitals of women with wool, it restrains and heals the red humor flowing from the womb.

### The name of this herb is Bucalion. (No. 20)

**This** herb, Bucalion, some call Calca. It has a soft stalk. The leaves are...

---

...similar to [little birds?]. It has a saffron-colored flower, similar to an eye, from which it received its name. It grows near the walls of cities. The leaves of this, crushed and applied with wax, dissolve bruises and hardnesses. The juice is expressed and, if drunk, restores natural color to those with jaundice <note>original: "ictericis"</note>, if they drink it three times before exiting a very hot bath.

### The name of this herb is Asterion. (No. 17)

**This** herb has minute and downy leaves. From one root it sends out many branches. it has a small dark flower and a saffron one similar to Bucalion. <margin>that is, <term>asterion</term> <note>meaning "star-like"</note></margin> If one grinds it with myrtle and it is diluted with oil, and stored in oil: he who has withdrawn with a sudden onset of shivering fever should be anointed; it will be healed. All these things, sifted and a spoonful taken in hot water, heal "hydrophobes" <note>original: "ydofobas", those fearing water due to rabies</note> and the bite of a mad dog. If a bundle is boiled in smith's milk <note>water used by blacksmiths to cool iron, thought to have medicinal properties</note>, half in the morning and half in the <margin>evening</margin> in equal parts, and then drunk: it heals the splenetic <note>those with spleen ailments</note>.

From this, a spoonful of sifted powder [mixed with wine?], if a woman drinks it after her menstrual purgation and lies with her husband, she will conceive immediately.

### The name of this herb is Hypiris. (No. 18)

**This** herb some call *anasistim*. It grows in damp places. It has a soft, reddish, rough stalk, with joints placed at intervals which are separated like [small bundles]. Along these joints, certain hairs curve in, which are thin and soft, and grow to a great height. It hangs down and is similar to a horse's tail; and therefore it is called <term>hypires</term> <note>from the Greek for horse; commonly known as Horsetail or Equisetum</note>. The root of this herb is hard, woody, and its power is styptic <note>contracts tissues to stop bleeding</note>. Therefore, the juice restrains the flow of blood from the nostrils if it is poured in or introduced with a flock of wool. Likewise, drunk with wine, it profits those with dysentery and moves the urine. Its leaves, if applied in the manner of a plaster, heal festering wounds. Indeed, the root of it and the herb itself, crushed and sifted and drunk in hot water to the measure of a spoonful: [heals] a cough...
