...it benefits those with shortness of breath; likewise, it aids those whose small veins within the throat have ruptured, and those who suffer from a foul odor in the armpits. It is also beneficial for those whose bladder has been ruptured <note>likely referring to ulcerations or stones rather than a literal rupture</note>. It heals them perfectly.

### The name of this herb, number 24, is Aizos.

<margin>Some call it *Ayzoos*.</margin>

**T**his herb, *Aizos* <note>original: "Aizos"; likely a species of Sempervivum or Houseleek</note>, grows on walls, rocks, and in mountainous, shady places, as well as among tombs. It sends out flat, low-lying branches from a single root, full of leaves that are small, long, sharp, fleshy, and succulent, arranged densely in the manner of ears of grain. From there, when it bears seed, it grows <unclear>upright</unclear> and long <unclear>stalks</unclear>, on the heads of which it bears very small, whitish flowers. The seed is also very tiny. It has an astringent and cooling power. Indeed, the root of this plant is discarded. Furthermore, the herb itself, crushed with pearl barley and applied in the manner of a poultice, soothes "sacred fire" <note>erysipelas, a skin infection</note>, shingles, burning in the eyes, burns, and the pain of gout. Its juice, mixed with rose oil and applied, eases a headache. The same herb, crushed with wine, is a potent drink against the bites of venomous spiders <term>spalangionum</term> <note>referring to the Mediterranean Black Widow or similar spiders</note>. It also heals dysentery, menstrual flow, <term>corimmia</term> <note>likely a corruption of a term for intestinal ailments</note>, and intestinal worms. For a discharge from a woman's womb, the juice applied with wool provides relief and restrains the flow. Used in the same way, it also relieves and heals eye pains.

### The name of this herb, number 25, is called Chamallos.

<margin>Some call this herb *Dertum*; others call it *Cacociana*.</margin>

**T**his herb has leaves very similar to the <term>myrtice</term> <note>tamarisk or myrtle-like shrub</note>, though somewhat broader and firmer, sharp at the tip, and thick. It sends out branches from the root that spread out along the ground. It bears fruit similar to a hazelnut, with a biting taste. It grows in rough places. This herb has the same power in all things that we have written for the herb known as *Chamalla* <note>likely Chamaeleon, a type of thistle</note>.

---

### The name of this herb, number 26, is called Heliotroptios.

**T**his herb is called *Heliotroptios* <note>Heliotrope</note> because its flowers turn toward the course of the sun. Others call it "Scorpion-herb," and the Romans call it "Wild Turnip." The juice of this herb, mixed with seventeen grains of pepper and given as a drink for three days, is a proven remedy for pain of the spleen. For headaches caused by <unclear>excessive heat</unclear>, the head and forehead should be pricked <note>or perhaps rubbed</note> with it. For <term>canthas</term> <note>possibly eye-corner sores or infections</note>, a mixture is made as follows: ten drams of spikenard, one ounce and five <unclear>scruples</unclear> of honey, four drams of <term>benozabif</term> <note>likely a corrupted name for a resin or spice</note>, four drams of cassia, and four drams of pepper. These are ground with old wine, and from this—along with three spoonfuls of the aforementioned herb—make lozenges of two scruples each, and give them with wine to drink.

### The name of this herb, number 27, is called Scolymbos.

<margin>from nature</margin>

**T**his herb, *Scolymbos* <note>Golden Thistle or Artichoke</note>, has a stem full of thorny leaves, at the top of which it bears thorny globes. It has a sturdy, black root. It is more effective than almost all other herbs for healing. This herb, boiled in wine and drunk, removes the foul odor of the armpits and the whole body. Furthermore, it draws out foul-smelling urine and provides a healthy food for country folk.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of medicinal plants from the Pseudo-Apuleius Herbarium. The previous page likely concluded the entry for the thirty-sixth herb. This page focuses on Yarrow (Achilleia), Figwort (Scrophularia), Chameleon, and Hart's Tongue Fern (Splenon).</meta>

# XXXVII The name is called Achilleia.

<margin>of the scribes</margin>
**H**owever, this herb is called "thousand-leaf" <note>original: "mille folium"; modern Yarrow</note> by some. By others, it is called *aymo fion* or *ambrosia*. Yet some others call it *chiliophyllon* <note>Greek for "thousand-leaf"</note>. The Gauls call it <unclear>oquin</unclear> *fiando ingencio*. The inhabitants of Ithaca call it *rhodola*. The Italians call it common *millefolium*, or "Venus's eyebrow," or "situated wax-plant." Indeed, Achilles discovered this herb <note>According to legend, Achilles used yarrow to heal the wounds of his soldiers at the siege of Troy</note>. It grows in cultivated places; it has golden and white flowers. The foliage of this herb, when crushed and applied to wounds, takes away the pain if they are fresh, and it glues them together. It binds a flow <margin>of blood</margin> of blood, and it assists the wombs of women when they are overflowing. Furthermore, if the herb is decocted, the mere vapor of it, if one sits over it, restrains the flowing humors of women. The same water, when drunk, cures dysentery. It is called *Achilleia* because Achilles is said to have used this herb most frequently in healing wounds.

...steps. It sends out equal stalks. It has seed in visible pods in the manner of <gloss>black</gloss> wax. <gloss>And the rough dry</gloss> Cydonian <note>likely referring to a quince-like quality</note> white... with a bitter taste. Fifteen grains of this herb, drunk beforehand with honey-water, are known to purge the humors of those with chest abscesses <term>empycos: those suffering from internal suppuration or empyema</term>; after a little while, the patient should walk about before vomiting. However, because of the thick name <note>likely a scribe's error for "throat"</note>, one ought to sip honey-water constantly, lest the juice of the herb burn the throat and cause choking. Furthermore, the seed itself, when used with purslane and oil of radish, heals itches and the scabies if rubbed on the body. When the seed is cast into food, it draws down humors and purges the brain. The seed itself is cooked in vinegar, and that vinegar cures toothache, defects of the gums, and rot in the mouth.

---

### The name of this herb XXXVII is called Scrophularia <note>Figwort</note>

### This is Camelleon or Cussicolis

**T**his herb, *Camelleon*, is called as if it were a wild olive, which some call *Turbiscon*. From the bottom of the turf, many stalks spring up; it has leaves similar to the olive...

---

...sinews, and frequent faces <note>likely "fauces" or throat</note>, with a taste like vinegar, so that it burns the throat. The leaves themselves are not only used, but mixed with honey-water: with wormwood likewise added in double amount, they are gathered into pills, which when swallowed whole, dissolve the bile. It draws out phlegm with bile without any torment. Indeed, if one applies the leaves of the same with white-lead and honey, it not only cleanses wounds but leads them to scarring.

### The name of this herb XXVIII is called Alcibiadides

**T**his herb is called *Alcibiadios* because its head and its seed are similar to an eyebrow. Its leaves are long, rough, and "venomous" <note>likely meaning potent or stinging</note>. It is bristling with very small thorns. It is inclined to send out many stalks among the leaves; the flowers are purple, below which is the seed, very similar to the head of a viper. Its root is small and black. About one drachm of this, drunk with wine, works against ser- <note>likely "serpents"</note>

### And the name is called Splenon or Scolopendrios <note>Hart's Tongue Fern</note>

**T**his herb is called *Splenon* because it soothes the spleen, for the reason that its leaves are <gloss>similar to the scolopendra</gloss> <note>a centipede</note> animal. This herb grows in stony and damp places; it has neither seed, nor stalk, nor flower. It lacks flowers. Its leaves are green on the surface, but reddish and woolly underneath. Vinegar in which the leaves of the same have been decocted, if drunk, cures the spleen. Also, when decocted in wine and crushed and applied as a plaster, or if the wine in which the herb was decocted is drunk, it relieves hysterics and the difficulty of urine in those who are burning. It singularly restrains stones in the bladder and crushes them; but this is only effective if it was collected when the moon is not visible, whether by day or by night. If collected before or after that time, it has no power. The same potion also relieves pain in the loins. Furthermore, it provides milk for the breasts. The same applies to both the herb, the root, and the seed.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of medicinal plants from the Herbarium of Pseudo-Apuleius, focusing on the Chamaeleon, Conyza, Red Bulb, and Dragonwort. The script is a clear Carolingian minuscule, typical of 9th-11th century medical manuscripts.</meta>

and with the spleen it is mild; for women, its herb is used on the face so they do not conceive. <note>original: "ne concipiant" - likely referring to a contraceptive or cosmetic use to prevent the 'mask of pregnancy'.</note>

# 42. The name of this herb is called Ciamallos. <note>original: "Ciamallos" - likely Chamaeleon (Chamaeleon gummifer).</note>

<margin>This herb is called Hebera; first it has "hebes-has" or "ulil" through "bre". <note>The marginalia appears to be a gloss on alternative names or scribal corrections.</note></margin>
**O**f this herb, the *camallos*, there are seven kinds. That which is the masculine type is called by some *ante* or *tam ualido*, but the female is called *miriceam* or *careren*. All have one power and are used for cataracts, although the shape varies. The juice of this herb is of a milky color; taken with one drachma <term>drachma: a Greek unit of weight, roughly 3.4 grams</term> of vinegar, it draws out phlegm and bile from the belly. If, however, it is taken with honey-water, it provokes vomiting. It is collected and dried in the summer; its pressed juice is stored in an earthen vessel. Some mix flour with this herb and make pills the size of a bean to be swallowed. If they irritate the throat, they should be wrapped in boiled honey. Two or three drops of its juice instilled into the ears are sufficient to move the bowels. The fresh juice, when poured on the head, thins an excess of hair, and it grows back thinner. But if applied seven times, nothing more will grow back. Likewise, if poured into the fistulas of any teeth, it does the same, provided it does not burn the tongue; let the fistula be blocked so the juice stays with the tooth. It also drives away carbuncles and swellings in the gums. Its seed, collected at the time of the harvest and taken with wine, resists the power of poisons; likewise, its leaves stored in leather for use provide the same benefit. Its juice smeared on wonderfully removes "lions" <note>likely *leontiasis*, a skin condition</note>, shingles, and ear pain. Also, its root, dried, crushed, and sifted, if swallowed, draws out phlegm and bile from the belly. While it is being prepared, it should not be handled with bare hands, lest the eyes be sprayed; care should also be taken not to touch the face with stained hands, because its touch burns everything as if by fire; the body should be protected with fat, oil, or wine.

# 43. The name of this herb is Chonizade. <note>original: "Chonizade" - Conyza or Fleabane.</note>

<margin>B</margin>
**T**his herb is said to have a sweet odor; if it is cut, the herb itself sends up an angular stalk of two cubits, on which the leaves are thick, thin, and sticky—let them be fatty. It has a flower similar to a hyacinth or a plane tree, and it is round and rather rough. The roots are long and boxwood-colored. The pressed juice, boiled down to the thickness of honey, is formed into pills which, when dissolved, soothe roughness of the throat. The same is provided by eating the raw root. The boiled juice is given with warm water to those suffering from a chronic fever. It cures pains of the chest and lungs, old ulcers, and kidney issues when taken with wine and raisin wine; for those who know the art, it settles their pains. The root itself, eaten or drunk, cures all irritations, and smeared on wounds, it heals them most perfectly; the decoction of the root provides the same if it is drunk, making the draught effective.

# 44. The name of this herb is called Red Bulb. <note>original: "Bulbus rufus" - likely a species of Muscari or wild onion.</note>

<margin>B</margin>
**T**his herb, the Red Bulb, is suitable for the stomach. There is also another herb called *Scilles* <note>Squill (Drimia maritima)</note>, which is bitter to the taste; this is even more useful for the stomach. Both have a warming property. Indeed, this food, when cooked, nourishes the blood and heals bloating. Nevertheless, it expels all evils that were previously conceived. This herb alone, or with honey, when applied, cures dislocations, gout, and skin eruptions. Placed upon the belly, it heals those with dropsy <term>dropsy: edema or swelling caused by fluid retention</term>. Applied with honey to the bites of dogs, it is a certain and sharp remedy. Mixed with pepper and applied with pomegranate rind, it restrains sweating. It settles stomach pain. When roasted, it wipes away *pitiras*—that is, dandruff of the head—and *achoras*—that is, the scabies which despoils the scalp. Mixed with honey, it cleanses freckles and the face. Ground with pearled barley, it heals bruised ears. Ground with roasted "sea-foam" <note>original: "alcionio" - likely cuttlebone or a marine mineral</note>, it cleanses spots from the face and black scars. Eaten with vinegar, it heals eruptions or rashes of the lower parts. However, it should not be eaten for more than seven days, lest it burn the nerves or cause convulsions.

<margin>B</margin>
**T**his herb has broad leaves having a likeness to ivy, with white spots; it has a stalk...

...roots with two various stalks in the manner of a serpent. The seed is at the head in the likeness of a cluster of grapes. To this breast <note>likely a reference to the shape of the plant part</note> comes the "colax fice ereceo" <note>original: "colax fice ereceo"; likely a corrupted botanical name or local term</note>. This root is round like a turnip. It grows in shady and moist places. Its seed, fried and pressed and mixed with oil, when dripped into the ears, heals their pain. Likewise, the same seed, when pure and inserted into the nostrils with wool, eats away at a polyp and a cancer in whatever part of the body it may be. A golden weight <note>term: "grama aurea" - a specific measurement of weight</note> of this same seed is critical; and when drunk with pitch, they prepare for a distension. The root of this same herb in <gloss>and honey and wine</gloss> <unclear>cuat</unclear> should remain. For when boiled <gloss>and he drinks the dry seed</gloss> and pulled out and eaten with honey, it heals those who have strained or ruptured themselves <gloss>that is, it causes vomiting and draws out phlegm</gloss>, those who are coughing, and those suffering from catarrh <note>a buildup of mucus in the nose or throat</note>. It dissolves and draws out the phlegm that has hardened in the chest. However, the dry root, ground and sifted, is taken with honey—one spoonful—and it provides the same effect. Furthermore, it moves the urine; taken with wine, it excites the belly. Its root, crushed with honey and a mixture of the root of white nettle, heals cancerous sores. Suppositories made from it cure fistulas, drawing out the menses of women and aiding in childbirth. If anyone rubs his hands with the root of this herb <margin>α . n .</margin> <note>marginalia likely indicating a specific recipe or "nota"</note>, he can catch serpents very easily without any danger. The same root, ground with vinegar and smeared on the body, wipes away dark spots. Its leaves also heal fresh wounds. The same boiled with wine heals paronychia <note>original: "perimones"; infections around the fingernails</note>. If the eyes are anointed with the juice of the root, it dispels their dimness.

### The name of this herb (46) is called Meconem; by some, however, it is called Wild Poppy.

<margin>α . n .</margin>
**T**here are three kinds of this poppy herb. The power of all of them is one: both cooling and sleep-inducing. These are only annuals; they are boiled, and from the water itself, those who suffer from sleeplessness, if they wash their face, they fall asleep. The same decoction does the same if drunk. The heads of the herb itself, with pearled barley and applied as a poultice, make a health-bringing force. It is used for "holy fire" <note>Erysipelas, a skin infection</note> and all inflammations. For medicinal use, the poppies themselves should be gathered and kept while they are green. And then they should be dried in the shade and stored. When it is necessary, they are mixed with water and barley. Furthermore, this medicine is made from it: take the poppy with its leaves in oil and boil it down to half, and let the residue be thrown away; and from that strained water, mixed with honey, let it be stored for medicinal use. For this, taken from a small vessel, works against all internal pains, coughs, and fluxes, and is a moistening wash for the throat. It represses pain of the arteries or black bile, and the hardness of the belly, as well as dysentery. The seed of the same herb mixed with black [poppy] and drunk in wine: it stops and constricts the flux of the belly and the moisture of the womb. The same ground with water and placed on the forehead: it represses wakefulness and insomnia. If indeed the juice is pressed from the head of the poppy itself and boiled to the thickness of honey, it has such power that one pill made from it to the size of a chickpea, dissolved in warm water and drunk, provides all the things contained above. If, however, more is taken, it induces sleep even unto the danger of death. In the same way, if it is mixed in oil and the head is anointed with it, it will be healed from pain. It also thoroughly heals aching ears if it is dripped in mixed with almond oil. Boiled with old wine and mixed with saffron, it settles pains and inflammations of the eyes with its poultice. Diluted with vinegar, it heals "holy fire." For those with gout, it is beneficial with woman's milk. Also, if you have it ground with woman's milk and saffron and mixed, you may make a salve from it. If it is administered through the anus, it induces sleep and is a cooling agent. The name of this herb is Colocindis, that is, Agria, which is number 46.

### Colocynth

**T**his same herb some call Wild Gourd. Some, indeed, call it "Afrigelelam." And similarly to the cucumber or the gourd, it stretches its vines along the ground. It has leaves similar to a cucumber; it has a fruit <margin>α . n .</margin> just like a bitter gall, which should be gathered at the time when it turns from green to pale yellow. It has a soft interior separated from the seed; a third part of a drachm <note>term: "dragme" - a Greek unit of weight</note>, that is, one scruple, mixed with water, ground and drunk: it purges the belly. Or with honey and pure wine and myrrh in an unboiled potion: it does the same. The dry gourds themselves are broken up and <gloss>that is, of wine</gloss> boiled in water; injected via a clyster <note>an enema</note>, they draw out bile and phlegm. The same ground and placed over the navel of pregnant women destroys the fetus <note>an abortifacient use</note>. If it is cleaned without the seeds and boiled in a pot: if it is put in the mouth, it settles the pain of the teeth. Lest it burn, it should be surrounded with chalk or clay. <margin>α . n .</margin>

### The name is called Hypericum.
<note>original: "ypericon". Commonly known as St. John's Wort.</note>

---

If drunk for forty days, know that it cures <term>sciatica</term><note>original: "sciatacă". Nerve pain in the leg.</note>; for the leaves, crushed with the seed and applied, cure burns. They bloom in the fourth and sixth months. The name of this herb: 48.

### The name is called Lapathus.
<note>original: "Lapatius". Commonly known as Dock or Sorrel.</note>

---

<margin>H</margin>
This herb is called <term>Corion</term><note>original: "Cori en". Likely referring to a variety of Hypericum or a similar plant.</note> because of its similarity, for its leaves are similar. Many small twigs spring from one tuft; they bear a flower similar to a violet. It has other oblong berries, the size of barley. In these is a black seed with the scent of resin. It grows in rough and cultivated places; crushed and drunk, it moves the urine. If it is placed under a woman as a <term>pessary</term><note>original: "ī appendi". A medicinal plug.</note>, it provokes the menses. Drunk with wine, it puts to flight <term>quartan fever</term><note>A fever that recurs every four days, often associated with malaria.</note>. Its seed crushed with wine.

<margin>B</margin>
There are four kinds of this herb, Lapathus. But the wild kind is the most effective. If it has any superior quality—that is, when it is understood to be well-moistened and full—the raw leaves of Lapathus, chewed and persistently applied to every pain, disperse a <term>collection</term><note>An abscess or gathering of fluid/pus.</note>. Its seed, crushed with wine or water, is effective for those suffering from dysentery, stomach ailments, and loss of appetite, and it is drunk usefully. The same potion also benefits those who have been struck by scorpions or who fear them. The raw root itself, boiled in vinegar and crushed in the same, heals leprosy, jaundice, and <term>scabby nails</term><note>Fungal infections of the nails.</note> when applied; but in this manner: first, let the body be rubbed in the sun with <term>nitre</term><note>original: "nitro". Naturally occurring sodium carbonate.</note> and vinegar for convenience. It also checks itching. Likewise, the roots boiled in wine, with the juice held in the mouth, soothe the pain of teeth. It dissolves <term>parotids</term><note>original: "Paritocidas". Swellings of the parotid glands, like mumps.</note> and <term>scrofula</term><note>original: "syadas". Swelling of the lymph nodes.</note> when the same roots are applied with wine. Boiled in vinegar, the herb itself relaxes hardness of the spleen. Applied to the nature of a woman <note>The genitals</note>, it restrains the flow of humors. There is an herb, <term>roginia</term>, which if drunk boiled with water, purges. The same potion breaks bladder stones. It also provokes the menses of women.
The name of this herb: 49.

### Heliotropium.

nor do they grow afterward. For <term>scabies</term><note>A contagious skin disease.</note> of the body, you shall burn Heliotrope upon a clean plate and collect its ash and temper it with vinegar; then lead the man into a bath, and while he is sweating, prick him. When you have rubbed the man well with this medicine, you may also smear a beast of burden with the same medicine. If you give it to a <term>falling-sick person</term><note>original: "Caduco". <margin>Of this herb Quincquage</margin>

---

->Sea Onion<-
<note>original: "Squilla"</note>

**B**ehold, there are two kinds of this herb. It has a thinning power. If it is the field variety, which is red, it ought to be avoided; but the mountain variety is better, which is called the White herb. It ought to be collected in this month. It is useful if cooked in an oven covered in polenta or clay, as if in coals. Meanwhile, let the sharp bitterness be checked thus: if it is not yet cooked, cover it again with another covering until it reaches softness, for they are harmful if not thoroughly covered. Furthermore, it is cooked better in nothing than in a very fine earthen vessel. And buried under a vacuum of dung and protected by ashes, let it be prepared in the middle part just like a decoction in the same water; let the water be made so that it is not bitter, and let it cook in that. Afterward, let the parts be placed on wood through the lower openings so they do not rot, and let them be dried in the shade. For if you mix one certain part of this with salty... and three parts of water... and let this be an ointment for the eyes <gloss>that is</gloss> to be applied to all things. And let it draw out when he drinks for the belly. This also provokes urine. It profits scrofula and stomachs <gloss>that is / internal</gloss> in which food floats due to over-digestion <gloss>that is / prefers</gloss>. It leads the weight of the stomach to the lower parts. It heals jaundice. It heals torments of the intestines, and it profits those with shortness of breath and those expelling phlegm. And without salt, let one drachma be taken, then let it be diligently cooked mixed with honey and taken by the spoonful. It does all the same things, and especially profits the stomach for the digestion of food. This should be given to those who have no outward life <note>perhaps meaning those who are extremely weak or bedridden</note> so that it does not harm them. For children, with saffron...

...until it in no way smears the same excrements. If you roast the <unclear>middle</unclear> part of the raw plant and apply it to fistulas... for let us place the herb itself with honey as we have received for figs <note>likely hemorrhoids or polyps</note>, neither loosened. To eat it raw is a remedy for the same. With oil and resin, it is said to glue together "ragados" <note>fissures/cracks in the skin</note> and heals bites and wasps. Also, this same herb, if hung at the threshold of any house, drives away all evil things.

Summer
</margin>
...summer. The stem of this plant is dry or white, as well as green. At the top, rough and thorny little balls are born. The root is long and black on the outside, with a good scent. It grows in fields and in rough places. By its power and properties, this plant, when crushed and drunk with wine: moves the urine and provokes menstruation. It dissolves griping pains of the belly and bloating. It is also effective for those with liver complaints and for snake bites. Furthermore, it helps many internal organs if it is rubbed on with the seed and oil. This same plant, for another reason and <gloss>or from here</gloss> applied as a plaster <note>original: "cathaplasmate"</note>, tempers all swellings and dissolves humors. If the roots of this herb are gathered at the summer solstice before sunrise and boiled in oil until they lose their color therein—so that they are able to crack—then the roots are thrown out. Let them be put into <unclear>the same</unclear> oil and most diligently ground with silver dross <note>litharge or lead oxide</note>. Then, let it be stirred diligently and evenly with a small broken wooden spatula, so that it does not sink by its own weight before mixing. Then let it be stored in a ceramic jar. It will provide wonderful help against the sting of scorpions and all snakes, as well as the bite of a rabid dog, if the wound is first cut with iron and this plaster is applied; provided that the sick person does not catch the scent of it. This also treats skin eruptions <note>original: "impetigo"</note> and cools the "sacred fire" <note>likely Erysipelas, a skin infection</note>. If it is applied when the pain of gout begins to come on, it brings relief and is a proven remedy.

### The name of this fifty-fourth herb is called Hera.

---

**This herb**
they call Verbena <note>Vervain</note>. <gloss>That is, from the Greeks</gloss> it received this name because priests were accustomed to use it in purifications. Its form: from a single sod of wine-colored earth, shoots rise up. They are about two cubits long, and many are larger. Both are good, knotted in several places. And from these knots, leaves are born: narrow and entirely jagged, with a slightly sweet taste. It has a long and thin root. The whole plant itself, crushed in wine with its roots, heals snake bites, both when applied to the wound and given as a drink. It also benefits those with jaundice <note>original: "arquatis"</note>. Let a drachm of the leaves—that is, three scruples—be ground with a grain of incense and a pound of warm wine, and given to one fasting at a crossroads <note>a common ritual site in folk medicine</note>. Its crushed leaves, applied in the manner of a plaster, mitigate tumors and the heat of old wounds. It purges foul wounds and nourishes them until they scar. Also, for rot of the mouth and throat: the whole herb boiled in wine and used as a gargle does not allow the rot to creep into the internal parts. Furthermore, it heals tertian fevers; if three are suddenly pulled from one sod, they help; for quartan fevers, four are beneficial.

### The name of this fifty-fifth herb is called Strucion.

---

**This herb**
**they call**
**Lanana** <note>Soapwort, used for washing wool</note>, and
because of which many wash wool with it. Its root is bitter and diuretic. It moves the urine; the powder of this herb in honey-water heals the spleen. It also benefits those with liver trouble, those coughing, those short of breath, and those with uterine issues, and it purges the belly. The same, drunk with the root of panax and capers in honey-water, dissolves and provokes bladder stones. It dissolves hardness of the spleen. When ground with pearled barley and vinegar, it heals leprosy. Likewise, cooked with barley meal and wine, it disperses all hardnesses and swellings. The powder of the same, put into the nostrils, creates sneezing. The same, infused with honey in the nostrils, purges the head, provided the mouth is open and the face is prone toward the ground.

### These herbs are called Delphinon:

because its seed or flower is similar to a dolphin; it has a flower like a pomegranate blossom. Its leaves are similar to those of silverweed <note>original: "archemetie"</note>, though smaller. The juice of this, collected and with a grain of pepper in one part—that is, on the first day, eight grains; on the third day, eight grains. If you give this to one suffering from quartan fever before the onset of the fit, they will be freed with wonderful speed.

### The name of this fifty-seventh herb is called Centimorbia.

->The name of this fifty-eighth herb:<-
->It is called Golden Violet.<- <note>original: "Viola aureola"</note>

**O**f this herb there are three main kinds: purple, white, and honey-colored. The honey-colored one is most suitable for medicine. This, when boiled and placed steaming hot over a seat, relaxes and provokes the bowels. Its leaves, crushed and mixed with vinegar, heal rashes. It is effective for a migraine of the head. You should anoint the arches of the head with its juices and nard <note>an aromatic essential oil</note>, and the migraine hairs <note>likely referring to the scalp/temples</note> will remain [healthy] at all times. For the color and pain of the whole body, a suitable ointment is prepared thus: seventeen <term>drachms</term> <note>a unit of weight, approx. 3.4 grams</note> of marjoram <note>original: "Sanfuco"</note>, fifty drachms of dried roses, forty-five drachms of <unclear>pficinum</unclear>, forty drachms of <unclear>Fryssillitira</unclear>, forty drachms of wax, eight drachms of ammoniac, eight drachms of sweet clover <note>original: "Mellilora"</note>, eleven drachms of Spanish oil. You shall mix the waxes with the juice and cook it until it reaches the thickness of honey, and use it so it becomes a firm ointment. And for a drink to relieve poisons, even if one has already imbibed them, you will be freed <margin>i n</margin> immediately by this mixture: two drachms of myrrh, ten drachms of oil, two drachms of dill, four drachms of saffron, eight drachms of <unclear>Corun</unclear>, eight drachms of <term>stacte</term> <note>oil of myrrh</note>, and nine drachms of <unclear>exteri arteri</unclear>, one ounce of vinegar, and sea water. You shall make two portions of saffron in wine; if you wish, do so with the herb savin.

**T**his other herb has round leaves: a purple flower and honey-colored seed. The herb is hot and <unclear>Causffead</unclear>. For pain of the spleen: the root of the Caper, crushed and sifted, makes an ounce of poultice. Placed upon the spleen, it dries it out. If you bind it to a man: lest he eructate <note>belch</note> sorrowfully from the pain. And after three hours, lead the sufferer into a bath, and make him sit upon a stool; then, while he is unaware, you push him down <note>into the water</note>, and he will be freed.

<margin>Anagallis</margin>

**The Benefit** of this herb, Anagallis: its juice mixed with honey in equal weights and poured into the nostrils will draw out phlegm. It is also taken to soothe the pain of teeth. If its juice is mixed with rose oil and the face is anointed therewith, one is looked upon with favor by all. So that a woman may have a clear and bright face, the juice of the herb Anagallis in honey

and mixed with bryony and very sharp vinegar: if you mix these, from this a woman may prick <note>or dapple</note> her face, and she shall have a clear and bright face. For epileptics and those who are alienated in mind: let the juice of the herb Anagallis be mixed with honey and injected into the nostrils; it purges the head. It draws back a great spreading skin-disease <note>original: "epistem"</note> with honey; the herb Anagallis is used for removing blemishes from the face as much as is sufficient to be taken. Of *Alonionum* <note>possibly a mineral salt or sea-foam derivative</note> and sea-foam, 6 drachms; of pumice, 8 drachms; of salt-yeast, 1 drachm; of soda <note>original: "nitrum"</note>, 2 drachms; and of *tristitrice* <note>an unidentified ingredient</note>, 6 drachms; of salt, 6 drachms; mix with the juice of the plant. And when one wishes to be cured, first purge the face with soda, and they shall be freed from all their blemishes.
The name of this herb is **Panacia** <note>Panax or All-heal</note>.

<margin>Panacia</margin>

**The Benefit** of this herb: its leaves, crushed and mixed in honey, heal the cancer of the teeth by which teeth fall out. Two drachms of *Seinmus* <note>possibly a corruption of a seed name</note> with soda, crushed with wine and prepared with honey, and applied to the genitals, moves the menses and casts out a dead fetus. <margin>Purple Violet</margin>

The name of this herb is **Purple Violet**.
**The Benefit** of this herb, the Purple Violet: its leaves pounded with an equal weight of lard <term>axungia: rendered animal fat used as an ointment base</term> heal fresh and old wounds most effectively. They also dissolve all inflammatory swellings. Its flowers, however, placed in honey and moistened with the best wine, wonderfully heal the heat of the stomach. This herb, the "foul" violet <note>perhaps referring to a specific wild variety</note>, is cooling.

<margin>Male Zamalencion</margin>

In stony places.
For this herb, **Zamalencion** <note>likely a variant of Chamaleon</note>, grows in stony or mountainous places. This herb, well-crushed with lard without salt and applied to wounds, heals every wound and cancerous sores. This herb, dried and reduced to a very soft powder, cleanses all cancerous wounds. The name of this herb is called Male Zamalencion.

<margin>Sison</margin>

**This** herb grows in stony places. For the wounds of men, take this herb, pound and grind it with lard, and when applied, it smooths every wound and leads it to a scar.

By the Latins it is called *Laber* <note>likely *Sium latifolium* or Water Parsnip</note>. Others have said it is called *uisidem*. It grows in watery places. Its leaves are smaller than those of the *Clyfacro*. In taste, it is aromatic. Whether chewed, boiled, or raw, it crushes bladder stones and draws out urine. It moves the menstrual flow of women. It wonderfully aids those suffering from dysentery.

The name of this present herb is called *Lycanis* <note>original: "Lycanis", likely *Lychnis* or Rose Campion</note>.
**T**his herb, *Lycanis Stephanice*, has oblong and narrow leaves, woolly and whitish. It has a stem with similar woolly parts; at the very top, it has small husks from which a violet flower <unclear>emerges</unclear>.

<column-break/>

For the seed of this plant, when given with wine, is beneficial against the bites of all serpents and the stings of scorpions.

<margin>Some men affirm it has such power through wisdom that if they place it upon scorpions, the creatures immediately receive a stupor—that is, a numbness—from which they die.</margin>

This herb *Lycanis* has such great power—

<column-break/>

The name of this herb is called *Abrotanum* <note>Southernwood, a flowering plant in the wormwood family</note>. Some indeed...

The Greeks call it this; the Latins or the goddesses <note>original: "deas", possibly a scribal error for "docti" (the learned) or a reference to Artemis</note> call it *Abrotanum*.

**O**f this herb *Abrotanum* there are two kinds: female and male. The female is branched with very tiny

leaves, so that it looks like a hair comb <note>original: "pecten capillamenti"</note>. The flowers of the female are very tiny and golden, of a good scent, and bitter with a heavy taste.

<page-num>82</page-num>

Its seed, ground with water, is effective for those suffering from <term>procacuum</term> <note>likely a corruption of 'profluvium' or similar discharge</note>, the suspicious, and those with sciatica, as well as those who have difficulty urinating. Taken as a drink, it helps the paralyzed. To those for whom a favorable condition is not given, it moves the menses. It aids against poisons and the bites of serpents when drunk in wine; for if it is ground with oil and smeared over the body, it brings relief. It is beneficial against cold fevers. It also puts serpents to flight by its scent or when burned. Likewise, it is effective against the stings of the <term>phalangion</term> <note>a venomous spider</note> or scorpions. The burning <unclear>parts</unclear> with honey <note>original: "echidonen uotam"</note>, crushed with bread and applied as a <term>cataplasm</term> <note>a warm, moist medicinal poultice</note>, mitigates boils and <term>spangre</term> <note>possibly carbuncles or gangrenous sores</note>; decocted with barley meal, it achieves the same. Indeed, the same herb which is called the "male"—

—three fingers long, octagonal. It has rough leaves surrounding the stem, arranged at intervals; a white flower. The seed is hard, round, and hollow in the middle, in the manner of a <unclear>milk-vessel</unclear>. The juice of this herb, drunk with wine, is a remedy against the bites of spiders and vipers. The same juice of the herb itself, dropped into the ears, cures their pain. It heals.

### Here begins the Letter of Apollinaris
### On Boxes and On Plasters...

<margin>
This herb, however, is called Aparine, or "the friend of man" <note>original: "phylantropos"</note>, or "Onpiloncarp," because it clings to people's clothes. But for that reason, the Romans call a similar herb "lappa" <note>Burdock or Cleavers</note>. [Also called] Barnos molaenure.
</margin>

One and a half pounds of silver dross <note>litharge</note>. One ounce of white lead. One ounce of wax. And a fourth part of <term>lacc aunteris</term> <note>likely a resin or lacquer</note> which first had the "male" quality. One iron [vessel]. Oil. [Add] chickpeas... in fried resin, two ounces. A drop of mountain <unclear>silver</unclear> with opium... all these things above... in the ninth meal... as [described] below. First, press the oil to soften the living [ingredients]. When it begins to grow hot, throw in the silver dross, well-ground and sifted; move it constantly with a wooden spatula until it appears well-mixed, and make it like a <term>malagma</term> <note>a softening ointment</note>. Then sprinkle the white lead, similarly sifted, over it and do not cease to stir.

---

Then, having made the wax into small pieces, and when it has dissolved, remove it from the coals. <gloss>Then</gloss> [add] the fried resin, sifted; <gloss>redly</gloss> indeed, the <gloss>juice</gloss> of <term>ammoniacum</term> <note>a medicinal gum resin</note> should follow, put in well-sifted. Then put in the galbanum [crushed] with a pestle, with half an ounce of wax from the aforementioned weight, thickened because it cannot be entered [otherwise]. Having put this in, return the pot to the soft coals until everything is united. When all is poured out, move it from the pot into a mortar. Put the opium, ground with milk, into the mortar <gloss>to soften it</gloss> once it has been infused. And when it has been mixed into one mass, well-blended, leave it in the mortar or wherever you wish, so that when it is needed as a plaster of the art, you may use it to soothe whomever you wish.

### On darkening
### the hair.

Take as much as is sufficient of the dung of a <unclear>dog</unclear>; mix it with boiled vinegar and put it in an earthen vessel. Keep it buried in horse manure for thirty-five days, and thus let it macerate. Smear this medicament on a shaved head in the shade using a painter's brush, but first anoint the face with a healthy <term>cerate</term> <note>a wax-based ointment</note> so that nothing drips from there and stains. And while it dries in the <unclear>sun</unclear>, anoint with oil... lest it becomes too dark. After you have <unclear>finished</unclear>, wash the head on the fourth day. Dove droppings <gloss>fresh</gloss>, if anyone grinds them with vinegar and places them on the head, heals all <term>alopecia</term> <note>patchy hair loss</note> and scars.

### How you may make the hair red.
### [Also called] Barde, or "haleeneya."

The <term>tassos</term> of a donkey—that is, the hard callus <note>original: "allexione"</note> which is commonly called "fielersa" found around its legs—you should burn and reduce to ashes. Apply that powder with oil and vinegar. This is so strong that if a woman anoints her breast with it, you would make her bearded.
